% Capture of generated supplementary tables so that the SWEVO main text can place some of them (\SuppTable{<label>}).
% Copied from SWEVO_supplement.tex (same macro names; no numbering changes).
\makeatletter
\newcount\supp@n
\def\supp@labels{}
\long\def\supp@getlabel#1\label#2#3\supp@stop{\def\supp@thislabel{#2}}
\newcommand{\supp@store}{%
  \global\advance\supp@n\@ne
  \expandafter\supp@getlabel\BODY\label{supp-unlabelled-\the\supp@n}\supp@stop
  \expandafter\global\expandafter\let\csname supp@body@\supp@thislabel\endcsname\BODY
  \xdef\supp@labels{\unexpanded\expandafter{\supp@labels}\noexpand\do{\supp@thislabel}}}
\newcommand{\CaptureSuppTables}[1]{\begingroup
  \RenewEnviron{table}[1][]{\supp@store}\RenewEnviron{table*}[1][]{\supp@store}%
  \input{#1}\endgroup}
\newcommand{\SuppTable}[1]{%
  \@ifundefined{supp@body@#1}{\par\noindent\TBD{generated table \detokenize{#1} not found}\par}{%
    \@ifundefined{supp@used@#1}{\global\@namedef{supp@used@#1}{}%
      \begin{table}[!htbp]\@nameuse{supp@body@#1}\end{table}}{}}}
\newcommand{\SuppTableOpt}[1]{\@ifundefined{supp@body@#1}{}{\SuppTable{#1}}}
\newcommand{\IfSuppTable}[3]{\@ifundefined{supp@body@#1}{#3}{#2}}
\newcommand{\SuppOmit}[1]{\global\@namedef{supp@used@#1}{}}
% \SuppTableNote{<label>}{<text>}: append a note below a captured table (inside its float); no-op if absent
\newcommand{\SuppTableNote}[2]{\@ifundefined{supp@body@#1}{}{%
  \expandafter\g@addto@macro\csname supp@body@#1\endcsname{\par\smallskip{\footnotesize\raggedright #2\par}}}}
\newif\ifsupp@left
\newcommand{\IfSuppTablesLeft}[1]{\global\supp@leftfalse
  \def\do##1{\@ifundefined{supp@used@##1}{\global\supp@lefttrue}{}}\supp@labels
  \ifsupp@left #1\fi}
\newcommand{\SuppRemainingTables}{\def\do##1{\SuppTable{##1}}\supp@labels}
% \SuppTableAddLabel{<label>}{<alias>}: give a captured table a second label (e.g. the name under which a table moved
% from the main text refers to it); no-op if the table is absent
\newcommand{\SuppTableAddLabel}[2]{\@ifundefined{supp@body@#1}{}{%
  \expandafter\g@addto@macro\csname supp@body@#1\endcsname{\label{#2}}}}
\makeatother

\title{PSO-VNS: A Two-Phase Particle Swarm Optimization with Variable Neighborhood Search for Wind Farm Layout Optimization}

% Abstract: 239 words (rule: whitespace-separated tokens of the source after replacing ~ by a space, each inline formula $...$ counted as one word; 236 raw whitespace tokens of the source; PDF-text token count to be re-measured after compilation; SWEVO limit 250), no citations.
% CHECK (manual, 2026-10-04): direct 1-deg optimization, 8 cases, seeds 31-60: PSO-VNS first (rank 1.12), PSO-VNS - PSO -0.194 pp (analysis/rev3_fine.json, tab:S-sa-fine); IEA37 36 turbines: GA first among gradient-free methods, exact-gradient methods first overall (tab:S-sa-iea-pool);
% GA best on Horns Rev 1 at 30,030 also re-evaluated at 1 deg (+0.48 GWh/yr) and with PyWake NOJ (+0.43) (analysis/rev3_sites.json, tab:S-sa-hr-eval); constraint handling
% (analysis/rev3_constraint.json, tab:S-sa-constraint): 6 cases x 5 methods; Deb + box clipping changes the run-level order, projection keeps it and the conditional PSO-VNS - PSO dL -0.095 pp [-0.162, -0.037], PSO-VNS vs PSO all-run 0.561 ns
\begin{abstract}
The wind farm layout optimization problem (WFLOP) places turbines to minimize wake losses under boundary and spacing constraints. To solve it, we propose PSO-VNS, a new two-phase variant of particle swarm optimization (PSO) with variable neighborhood search (VNS). A standard PSO with constriction coefficients first moves whole layouts towards the small, nonconvex feasible region; the basic VNS then refines the best layout by derivative-free compass search. The sequential design adds one parameter and isolates the gain of the local search. PSO-VNS is compared with eight methods, among them PSO, a genetic algorithm (GA), salp-swarm algorithms and SLSQP, on four wind farm types: the Kusiak--Song benchmark (two wind data sets, circular farms of three sizes), blocks of the Horns Rev~1 and Lillgrund wind farms, and the IEA37 case study. All methods receive equal budgets and paired seeds. PSO-VNS ranks first on the benchmark, statistically level with PSO and GA, and gains 0.19 percentage points of wake loss over PSO when layouts are optimized with 1$^\circ$ direction bins (eight cases). It also ranks first on the Lillgrund block, on the Horns Rev~1 block at the smaller budget and on IEA37 with 16 turbines; GA is best on Horns Rev~1 at the larger budget and on IEA37 with 36 turbines. An ablation study shows that the PSO phase is the component that matters: replacing it by a salp-swarm phase or by random sampling of equal cost gives worse results.
\end{abstract}

\section*{Nomenclature}
\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{$}l<{$}@{\quad}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.72\textwidth}@{}}
B & Budget (number of objective evaluations per run)\\
C_T,\ K & Thrust coefficient; Jensen wake-spreading constant\\
D,\ R & Rotor diameter and radius, $D=2R$ (benchmark 77~m; Horns Rev~1 80~m; Lillgrund 93~m)\\
E_{\rm ideal} & Wake-free benchmark objective of one turbine\\
F_p & Penalized wake loss (minimized), penalty constant $\mu_{\rm p}$\\
g^{\rm b}_i,\ g^{\rm s}_{ij} & Boundary and spacing violations\\
h_0,\ h_{\min} & Initial and final step of the compass search\\
k_{\max},\ \delta_k & Number and radii of the VNS neighborhoods $\mathcal N_k$\\
L & Wake loss (objective without penalty)\\
\ell_{ij},\ \ell_{\min} & Distance between turbines $i$ and $j$; minimum spacing\\
m & Equivalence margin (pp of wake loss)\\
n & Number of decision variables, $n=2N$\\
N,\ N_p & Number of turbines; population size\\
N_\theta,\ N_s & Numbers of wind-direction and wind-speed bins\\
r & Farm radius\\
s;\ \zeta,\ \psi & Wind speed; Weibull shape and scale\\
T,\ T_1 & Iterations of a population method; iterations of Phase~1\\
\mathbf V^i,\ \mathbf P^i,\ \mathbf G & Velocity, personal best and global best of PSO\\
w,\ c_1,\ c_2 & Inertia weight and acceleration coefficients of PSO\\
\mathbf X & Layout vector $(x_1,y_1,\ldots,x_N,y_N)$\\
\alpha & Wake half-angle, $\alpha=\arctan K$\\
\overline{\Delta L} & Mean wake-loss difference between two methods (pp)\\
\theta & Wind direction (blowing towards, CCW from east)\\
\xi_1;\ \gamma,\ \phi,\ \chi & SSA step coefficient; Laplace step, its location and scale\\
\omega & Share of the budget $B$ given to Phase~1
\end{tabular}

% begin optA/sw/02_intro.tex
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}

Where the turbines of a wind farm are placed is decided once, at the planning stage, and this decision determines how much of the available wind energy the farm converts over its entire lifetime. An upstream turbine leaves behind a wake of slower and more turbulent air that reduces the power of the turbines downstream, so the layout sets the wake loss and thereby the gross energy yield, and shapes the collector system and grid connection~\cite{Srikakulapu2018,Hou2019}. As the wake loss is a share of the wake-free energy, a difference of a few tenths of a percentage point is, within a given wake model, a lifetime energy difference of that size. The wind farm layout optimization problem (WFLOP) places a given number of turbines in a site to minimize the wake loss subject to boundary and minimum-spacing constraints; we address only this wake-loss part of layout design, for farms of up to 36 turbines.

For swarm and evolutionary computation, the continuous WFLOP is also a representative constrained benchmark. Its $2N$ coordinates are continuous, the objective is multimodal and, with a top-hat wake, discontinuous; the constraints are a site boundary and $N(N-1)/2$ pairwise spacing constraints, so the feasible region is nonconvex and becomes a small part of the bounding box as $N$ grows; one evaluation is cheap enough for thousands of runs; and standard instances with published results exist, from the Kusiak--Song cases~\cite{Kusiak2010} to the IEA Wind Task~37 case studies~\cite{Baker2019}. These properties, which it shares with many engineering design problems, have made it a common test bed for new metaheuristics~\cite{Azlan2021}, including swarm methods such as particle swarm optimization (PSO)~\cite{Pookpunt2013,Asaah2021} and, more recently, metaphor-based algorithms such as the salp swarm algorithm (SSA)~\cite{Mirjalili2017,Rezk2019}. What such studies can show depends on how the comparison is run.

For the continuous WFLOP, a single method rarely does well in both of the tasks the problem poses. Reaching the feasible region from random layouts calls for a population that moves whole layouts at once, whereas the last improvements are small moves of individual turbines, which a contracted swarm makes only by chance; and the top-hat wake makes the objective discontinuous, so that gradients cannot be used for this refinement. PSO is a well-understood global search with few parameters, and VNS a derivative-free local search with few parameters; combining them has helped in scheduling, routing and localization~\cite{Tasgetiren2007,Marinakis2017,Gumaida2019}, but the embedded designs used there spend local-search evaluations in every iteration and have not, to our knowledge, been applied to the WFLOP. This motivates a simple sequential variant whose phases have fixed, exactly counted budgets and whose contribution can be measured (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-motivation}).

A new algorithm, including the one proposed here, is justified by a comparison with other methods, and its claimed advantage is only as reliable as that comparison. Guidelines for experiments with metaheuristics~\cite{Hooker1995,Derrac2011,BartzBeielstein2020,LaTorre2021} and critiques of metaphor-based algorithms~\cite{Sorensen2015,Aranha2022} both point to the same weak spots: reference methods run with poor or unreported settings, budgets that are not equal, constraint violations that are ignored in the ranking, and statistics that confuse the absence of a significant difference with equivalence. In the WFLOP, the GECCO layout competition~\cite{Wilson2018} and the IEA Wind Task~37 studies~\cite{Baker2019,Thomas2023} compared optimizers on a common objective, and reviews list many metaheuristics tested under differing budgets and settings~\cite{Azlan2021}; these studies compare methods as submitted or as published, not under one protocol (Section~\ref{sec:related}). Three issues matter here. First, budgets counted in iterations, or without the gradient evaluations of gradient-based solvers, are not equal. Second, infeasible layouts returned by penalty-based metaheuristics must be ranked consistently. Third, mean values without paired tests and multiplicity correction~\cite{Benavoli2016} may mislead, and a nonsignificant difference is not evidence of equivalence~\cite{Lakens2017}. For two-phase hybrids, a further question is whether the first phase contributes more than a random start would.

In this paper, we propose PSO-VNS, a new two-phase variant of PSO with the basic variable neighborhood search (VNS)~\cite{Mladenovic1997,Hansen2001}, a hybrid in the memetic sense~\cite{Neri2012}, for the continuous WFLOP. PSO-VNS runs the standard PSO, proposed by Kennedy and Eberhart~\cite{Kennedy1995}, with the constriction coefficients of Clerc and Kennedy~\cite{Clerc2002}, and then refines its best layout by VNS. We compare it with eight methods: PSO, the SSA with VNS (SSA-VNS), the SSA, the Laplacian SSA (LX-SSA), previously proposed by Solanki and Deep~\cite{Solanki2023}, differential evolution (DE), a genetic algorithm (GA), VNS alone and multistart sequential least-squares programming (MS-SLSQP). The comparison covers four wind farm types: Type~I, the discretized Kusiak--Song benchmark, with two wind data sets, one with the same wind-speed distribution in all directions and one with direction-dependent wind speeds, on circular farms of three sizes (68 cases); Type~II, a 16-turbine block of Horns Rev~1; Type~III, a $4\times4$ block of Lillgrund; and Type~IV, IEA37 Case Study~1 with 16 and 36 turbines. The contributions are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Algorithm.} PSO-VNS runs the standard PSO for a share $\omega$ of the budget and the basic VNS from its best layout for the rest (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-method}). The sequential design uses the swarm to reach the feasible region and the local search to refine one layout, gives both phases exactly counted budgets, adds a single parameter to those of PSO and VNS and, as its first phase reproduces a PSO run exactly, lets the gain of the local search be measured against PSO with the same seeds.
\item \emph{Evaluation.} The nine methods are run on the four wind farm types under one protocol, following established practice~\cite{Derrac2011,BartzBeielstein2020,LaTorre2021}: equal budgets in objective evaluations, counting the gradient evaluations of MS-SLSQP; seed-paired runs; a feasibility-aware ranking with an all-run endpoint, as in constrained evolutionary computation~\cite{Deb2000,Wu2017}; Friedman and Holm-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank tests; and an equivalence analysis~\cite{Lakens2017,Benavoli2017} with a post hoc margin of $\pm0.05$ percentage points (pp) of wake loss, decided at the seed level of the fixed benchmark, with case and cluster levels as sensitivity analyses (Section~\ref{sec:setup}). The individual elements are established; their joint use we have not found reported in WFLOP comparisons. All results are regenerated from a reproducibility package.
% CHECK-EXTRA [X39]/[X41]: equivalence and minimal margins at seed, case and cluster level
\item \emph{Performance of PSO-VNS by wind farm type.} On Type~I, PSO-VNS has the best average rank of the nine methods, followed by PSO and GA; it is not significantly different from these two and significantly better than the other six. Its mean difference to PSO lies within the margin at the seed and case levels, not at the scenario-cluster level, with case-level differences in both directions. On Horns Rev~1 (Type~II), PSO-VNS ranks first at 6,030 evaluations and GA at 30,030. On Lillgrund (Type~III), PSO-VNS ranks first at both budgets, although at 6,030 evaluations MS-SLSQP, feasible in all runs, is better in the paired test. On IEA37 (Type~IV), PSO-VNS ranks first with 16 turbines and GA with 36 (Sections~\ref{sec:farm-I}--\ref{sec:farm-IV}). When the search itself uses 1$^\circ$ direction bins (eight cases of Type~I), PSO-VNS stays first and its advantage over PSO, about 0.19~pp, exceeds the margin (Section~\ref{sec:direction}). As the PSO phase of PSO-VNS reproduces a PSO run exactly for a given seed, the comparison with PSO isolates the contribution of the local-search phase.
% CHECK-FINAL [C01]: PSO-VNS best average rank; [C12]: constriction PSO \NPSOPos; CHECK-EXTRA [X39]/[X42]: equivalent at seed and case level, not at cluster level (CR1, CR2, BM); [X43]: \NXHetBeyondPSOVNS and \NXHetBeyondPSO cases beyond +-m in each direction
\item \emph{Ablation.} Replacing the PSO phase of PSO-VNS by an SSA phase (SSA-VNS), an LX-SSA phase (LX-SSA-VNS) or random sampling of equal cost in the farm (RSD-VNS) or in its bounding square (RS-VNS) shows that the PSO phase gives a clear gain over random sampling, whereas the salp-swarm phases give no gain larger than the 0.05~pp margin over RSD-VNS, and LX-SSA-VNS, with LX-SSA as published, is worse than RSD-VNS. At 6,030 evaluations, the VNS phase acts essentially as one compass-search descent (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}).

% CHECK-DIAG [D08]-[D10], [D20]: first descent \NDDescentSharePSOVNS% of the Phase-2 gain; cycles \NDCycleEvalsPctPSOVNS% of Phase-2 evaluations for \NDCycleSharePSOVNS% of the gain
\item \emph{Constraint handling.} Under Deb's feasibility rules (six cases, five methods), box clipping changes the comparison; radial projection keeps the run-level order, the leading method and the conditional advantage of PSO-VNS over PSO, but not the significance of its all-run advantage (Section~\ref{sec:boundary}). Our conclusions therefore concern the implemented penalty and repair.

\end{enumerate}

\emph{Organization of the paper.} Section~\ref{sec:related} reviews the literature and Section~\ref{sec:model} states the problem model. Section~\ref{sec:prelim} presents the preliminaries (PSO, SSA and basic VNS), Section~\ref{sec:psovns-method} the proposed PSO-VNS and Section~\ref{sec:hybrid} the comparison hybrids and random-sampling controls. Section~\ref{sec:setup} describes the experimental setup. Sections~\ref{sec:farm-I}--\ref{sec:farm-IV} report the four wind farm types, Types~I--IV, each with its experimental setting, numerical results and statistical analysis. Section~\ref{sec:ablation} gives the ablation study of PSO-VNS, and Section~\ref{sec:discussion} the conclusions and future directions.

\label{sec:related}

\subsection{Wind Farm Layout Optimization with Metaheuristics}
\label{sec:related-wflop}
\emph{Discrete and continuous formulations.} Early studies placed turbines in the cells of a square grid and optimized the cell occupation with genetic algorithms (GAs)~\cite{Mosetti1994,Grady2005}; binary PSO with time-varying acceleration coefficients was applied to the same kind of grid~\cite{Pookpunt2013}. A grid makes the problem combinatorial but restricts the layouts that can be found. Kusiak and Song~\cite{Kusiak2010} formulated a continuous model with turbine coordinates in a circular farm, a discretized wind rose, a Jensen wake~\cite{Jensen1983,Katic1986} and explicit boundary and spacing constraints. This model, with two wind data sets~\cite{Kusiak2010,Bansal2017} and several farm radii, has become a common reference for metaheuristics and defines the benchmark used here (Section~\ref{sec:setup}). On it, or on close variants, ant colony optimization (ACO) and particle filtering~\cite{Eroglu2012,Eroglu2013}, biogeography-based optimization (BBO)~\cite{Bansal2017} and random search inside the feasible space~\cite{Feng2015} have been proposed, and GA, PSO, differential evolution (DE) and simulated annealing were compared on offshore layouts~\cite{Elkinton2008}; PSO has reduced wake losses in~\cite{Asaah2021}.

\emph{Newer metaheuristics and hybrids.} Later work used self-informed GAs~\cite{Ju2019}, an adaptive evolutionary algorithm combined with Monte Carlo tree search~\cite{Bai2022}, deterministic refinement techniques~\cite{Nagpal2021} and VNS for large offshore farms~\cite{Cazzaro2022}. Metaphor-based algorithms have also been applied; for example, the water cycle algorithm was compared with the SSA and the grey wolf optimizer on turbine placement~\cite{Rezk2019}, and the Laplacian SSA, previously proposed by Solanki and Deep~\cite{Solanki2023}, was combined with VNS in a preliminary, unpublished comparison of ours that motivated this study. Reviews list many such methods, often evaluated on the same classical wind scenarios but with differing budgets, settings and constraint handling~\cite{Azlan2021}.

\emph{Common benchmarks.} The GECCO layout competitions provided a common evaluation interface, and the top entries of the 2015 edition were compared in~\cite{Wilson2018}. The IEA Wind Task~37 case studies fixed a simplified Gaussian wake, site boundaries and wind roses~\cite{Baker2019,IEA37repo}, and eight methods, gradient-based, gradient-free and hybrid, each run by researchers experienced with it, reached different layouts of similar energy yield on a common problem~\cite{Thomas2023}. Both efforts compare methods \emph{as submitted}: each team chose its own settings and effort, so the results show what experts achieve with their methods, not how the methods compare under one budget and one protocol.

\emph{Gradient-based and model-based alternatives.} Gradient-based optimization with exact gradients reduces the cost of layout optimization considerably compared with finite differences~\cite{Guirguis2016}; pseudo-gradients offer a cheap alternative to exact gradients~\cite{Quaeghebeur2021}, and reduced parameterizations of the layout cut the number of design variables~\cite{Stanley2019}. Gaussian wake models~\cite{Bastankhah2014,Tao2020} make the objective smoother than the top-hat Jensen wake. On such smooth models, the cost of a gradient decides how far these methods scale: a forward-difference gradient of the $2N$ coordinates costs $2N+1$ objective evaluations, whereas an analytic or reverse-mode algorithmically differentiated gradient costs a small multiple of one evaluation, at the price of memory~\cite{Rodrigues2024}. With algorithmic differentiation, parallel evaluation of the flow cases and a heuristic initial layout, gradient-based optimization has been applied to farms of 500 turbines~\cite{Rodrigues2024}; stochastic gradient descent reduces the cost of each step by sampling the wind conditions~\cite{Quick2023}; and a heuristic start followed by gradient-based refinement handles inclusion and exclusion zones~\cite{CriadoRisco2024}. Surrogate models extend layout optimization to larger farms and higher-fidelity flow models~\cite{Yang2023,Wang2024,Li2025}. Graph neural networks, which represent a farm as a graph of interacting turbines and thus apply to any layout, have been trained to predict turbine powers~\cite{ParkPark2019} and wake flow fields~\cite{LiZhang2023}, and have served as surrogates in layout~\cite{ParkPark2019} and yaw optimization~\cite{LiRobert2024}. With large-eddy simulations as the flow model, Bayesian optimization with Gaussian-process surrogates has optimized layouts and wake steering without gradients~\cite{Bempedelis2024}.

\emph{Position of this study.} We use the Kusiak--Song benchmark in its continuous form (discretized only in the wind directions), and three further wind farm types: a Horns Rev~1 block, a Lillgrund block and IEA37 Case Study~1 (Sections~\ref{sec:farm-I}--\ref{sec:farm-IV}). Unlike competitions, all nine methods (PSO-VNS, PSO, SSA-VNS, SSA, LX-SSA, DE, GA, VNS and MS-SLSQP) are run by the same code under one budget in objective evaluations, which for the gradient-based MS-SLSQP includes its finite-difference gradients. Surrogate-based methods are outside the present pool: they need training data and are usually reported under their own budgets. Exact gradients need a smooth model~\cite{Guirguis2016,Thomas2023}, and the top-hat objective used here is discontinuous; SLSQP with the exact gradient is therefore tested only on the piecewise-smooth IEA37 model, with each gradient charged at its measured cost in evaluations (Section~\ref{sec:iea-grad}). The protocol is complementary to such methods: budgets that count every model evaluation, including gradient and training evaluations, seed-paired runs and equal-cost controls apply in the same way; for instance, a heuristic initial layout~\cite{Rodrigues2024,CriadoRisco2024} could be compared with random sampling of equal cost in the farm, as the first phases are here.

\subsection{Benchmarking Methodology for Metaheuristics}
\label{sec:related-bench}
Hooker~\cite{Hooker1995} distinguished competitive testing, which tells which algorithm wins, from controlled experiments, which tell why. Later guidelines turned this into practice: equal budgets counted in function evaluations, reported and justified settings of all methods, well-chosen reference algorithms and component analyses of new proposals~\cite{BartzBeielstein2020,LaTorre2021}. Platforms such as COCO~\cite{Hansen2021} standardize budgets, instances and performance measures for continuous black-box optimizers, but for artificial test functions; a constrained engineering problem such as the WFLOP must additionally decide how infeasible final solutions enter the comparison.

For the statistics, Dem\v{s}ar~\cite{Demsar2006} and Derrac et~al.~\cite{Derrac2011} recommended nonparametric tests over multiple problems: the Friedman test with post hoc comparisons of average ranks and pairwise Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, with a multiplicity correction such as Holm's~\cite{Holm1979}. Benavoli et~al.\ showed that post hoc tests based on mean ranks depend on the other methods in the pool~\cite{Benavoli2016}, which is why our primary tests of differences use case means, and advocated Bayesian analyses with a region of practical equivalence~\cite{Benavoli2017}; Carrasco et~al.~\cite{Carrasco2020} reviewed these classical and newer trends for swarm and evolutionary algorithms and gave practical guidelines. Campelo and Takahashi~\cite{Campelo2019} derived the numbers of instances and runs needed for a given power and accuracy. Equivalence testing with two one-sided tests (TOST) asks whether a difference lies within a margin of practical irrelevance~\cite{Lakens2017}, and so avoids reading a nonsignificant difference as evidence that two algorithms perform equally.

\emph{Position of this study.} We apply this practice to the WFLOP and combine three elements, each established elsewhere, that we have not found applied together in WFLOP comparisons: feasibility-aware ranking of penalty-based methods (qualification, then conditional quality), related to the feasibility rules of constrained evolutionary computation~\cite{Deb2000} and the feasibility-first ranking of the CEC constrained competitions~\cite{Wu2017}; equal-cost random-sampling controls for the first phase of hybrids; and equivalence analyses (TOST and a Bayesian signed-rank test) at three levels of inference, over seeds, over cases and over the six designed scenario clusters (Section~\ref{sec:setup}). We did not size the experiment by a power analysis as in~\cite{Campelo2019}; instead, for each equivalence statement, we report the smallest margin at which it holds, so that a reader can apply a different margin.

\subsection{Critiques of Metaphor-Based Algorithms}
\label{sec:related-metaphor}
S\"orensen~\cite{Sorensen2015} argued that many ``novel'' metaheuristics hide known search operators behind a new metaphor and are justified by comparisons with weak baselines; a group of editors and researchers later called for action on the proliferation of such methods~\cite{Aranha2022}. Camacho-Villal\'on et~al.~\cite{CamachoVillalon2023} showed for six popular metaphor-based algorithms that their search rules reuse known ideas under new names and are presented in a misleading way. For the SSA~\cite{Mirjalili2017}, the critical review of Castelli et~al.~\cite{Castelli2022} analyzed the update rules and the evidence for its performance; in the form used here, the leaders perturb the best solution uniformly with a shrinking radius and the followers only average (Section~\ref{sec:ssa}).

\emph{Position of this study.} These critiques rest mostly on the update equations or on artificial test functions. We add a component-level test on an applied constrained problem, as part of the ablation of PSO-VNS: whether a salp-swarm first phase does better than random sampling of equal cost in the farm (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}). This is also a self-critique, as LX-SSA was previously proposed by Solanki and Deep~\cite{Solanki2023}, the first of whom is an author of this study; the test assesses LX-SSA as published, including its cost of $2N_p$ evaluations per iteration, and an ablation separates its Laplace step from that cost (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}).

\subsection{PSO Parameterization}
\label{sec:related-pso}
The inertia weight~\cite{Shi1998} and the constriction factor of Clerc and Kennedy~\cite{Clerc2002} were introduced to control the divergence of the particles in the PSO of Kennedy and Eberhart~\cite{Kennedy1995}; the constriction setting is equivalent to an inertia-weight PSO with $w\approx0.73$ and $c_1=c_2\approx1.50$. Stability analyses of the particle dynamics~\cite{Poli2009,Cleghorn2018,Bonyadi2017} place this setting inside the region in which the mean and the variance of a particle's position converge (Section~\ref{sec:pso}); we therefore use the standard PSO with the constriction coefficients of Clerc and Kennedy, without tuning, for PSO and for the first phase of PSO-VNS.

\subsection{Hybrid Swarm--Local-Search Designs}
\label{sec:related-hybrid}
Combining a population-based global search with a local search is the idea of memetic algorithms; Krasnogor and Smith~\cite{Krasnogor2005} gave a taxonomy and discussed design issues such as the choice of the local search and its integration into the evolutionary cycle, and Neri and Cotta~\cite{Neri2012} reviewed memetic computing for discrete, continuous and constrained problems. VNS~\cite{Mladenovic1997,Hansen2001} changes neighborhoods systematically around an incumbent and has a continuous form~\cite{Mladenovic2008}; in the WFLOP, it has been used for large offshore farms~\cite{Cazzaro2022}, and deterministic refinement techniques for layouts have been compared in~\cite{Nagpal2021}. Outside wind energy, PSO and VNS have already been combined, with VNS as the local search of a PSO, for flowshop scheduling~\cite{Tasgetiren2007}, constrained shortest paths~\cite{Marinakis2017} and sensor-network localization~\cite{Gumaida2019}. The simplest of these designs is the sequential (relay) hybrid, which runs the global method for part of the budget and passes its best solution to the local method.

\emph{Position of this study.} PSO-VNS is a new two-phase variant of this kind of hybrid for the WFLOP. Unlike the PSO--VNS designs above~\cite{Tasgetiren2007,Marinakis2017,Gumaida2019}, which apply VNS within the PSO loop, it runs the two methods in sequence, with exactly counted budgets and a first phase that reproduces a PSO run (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-method}). Its evaluation includes an ablation: the first phase is compared with equal-cost random sampling in the farm and in its bounding square, and with the local search alone, so that the contribution of each phase is measured rather than assumed; and the second phase is instrumented, to show what the local search actually does at the given budget (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}).

% end optA/sw/02b_related.tex

% begin optA/sw/03_model.tex
\section{Problem Modeling}
\label{sec:model}

The problem has the following elements (Fig.~\ref{fig:model}).
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Decision variables.} For a prescribed number $N$ of identical turbines with rotor radius $R$ (diameter $D=2R$), the $n=2N$ coordinates of the layout $\mathbf X=(x_1,y_1,\ldots,x_N,y_N)$.
\item \emph{Objective.} The expected farm power is maximized, i.e., the wake loss is minimized, under the Jensen wake model~\cite{Jensen1983,Katic1986} and the power curve of~\cite{Kusiak2010} (Sections~\ref{sec:wind}--\ref{sec:powermodel}).
\item \emph{Wind direction.} The direction $\theta$ has the probability density $p_\theta$; it is the direction \emph{towards} which the wind blows, counter-clockwise from east (Fig.~\ref{fig:model}a). The meteorological directions $\theta_{\rm met}$ of Horns Rev~1 and IEA37 (wind \emph{from}, clockwise from north) are converted by $\theta=270^\circ-\theta_{\rm met}$.
\item \emph{Constraints} (Section~\ref{sec:constraints}). The turbine distances $\ell_{ij}=\lVert(x_i-x_j,\,y_i-y_j)\rVert_2$ are at least $\ell_{\min}=4D=8R$~\cite{Kusiak2010,Bansal2017}, and the farm is a disc of radius $r=500$, 750 or 1000~m.
\end{itemize}
The spacing $4D$ is the convention of the Kusiak--Song benchmark and is kept for comparability; it lies at the dense end of practice (the installed Horns Rev~1 layout is a regular grid of about $7D$; Section~\ref{sec:hornsrev-site}), so our results concern a packing-constrained regime (larger spacings: Section~\ref{sec:spacing}).

The model is the unchanged Kusiak--Song benchmark model, and no modeling novelty is claimed; the contributions are the PSO-VNS algorithm and its controlled evaluation. It rests on the following assumptions, each stated in detail in the subsections below:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[A1.] All $N$ turbines are identical, and their number is prescribed.
\item[A2.] The terrain is flat, and the farm boundary is a circle of radius $r$ (blocks of the Horns Rev~1 and Lillgrund farms: their own boundaries).
\item[A3.] Turbines must be at least $\ell_{\min}=4D$ apart.
\item[A4.] In each direction bin, the wind speed follows a Weibull distribution; the wind rose is discretized into $N_\theta$ direction bins.
\item[A5.] Wakes follow the Jensen top-hat model with a constant thrust coefficient $C_T$; a turbine is waked if its hub center lies in the wake cone, and the deficits of several wakes are combined by the root-sum-square rule.
\item[A6.] The power curve is that of the Kusiak--Song benchmark: linear between cut-in and rated speed, constant above.
\item[A7.] Only the wake loss is optimized; the electrical collector-system layout and multi-objective formulations (e.g., AEP against cost or the levelized cost of energy) are not considered.
\end{enumerate}
The protocol (equal budgets, seed pairing, equal-cost controls, feasibility-aware ranking, equivalence tests) does not depend on the model and applies to such formulations as well.

(a) Circular farm of radius $r$ and wind direction $\theta$; the points show, for illustration, a 12-turbine layout ($r=750$~m); discs of radius $2D$ touch at the spacing $\ell_{\min}=4D$. (b) Jensen top-hat wake behind a rotor of radius $R$: width $2(R+Kd)$ at the downstream distance $d$, uniform speed $s_{\text{down}}<s_{\text{up}}$. (c) Wake half cone of turbine $j$ ($K$ exaggerated): virtual vertex $A$ at $R/K$ upstream of the rotor, half-angle $\alpha=\arctan K$; turbine $i$ is waked by $j$ if $\beta_{ij}<\alpha$.}

\subsection{Wind Model and Direction Discretization}
\label{sec:wind}
For each direction, the wind speed $s$ has the Weibull density
\begin{equation*}
p_s(s;\zeta,\psi)=\frac{\zeta}{\psi}\Bigl(\frac{s}{\psi}\Bigr)^{\zeta-1}\exp\Bigl[-\Bigl(\frac{s}{\psi}\Bigr)^{\zeta}\Bigr]
\end{equation*}
with shape $\zeta(\theta)$ and scale $\psi(\theta)$. The benchmark rose is piecewise constant: $N_\theta=24$ direction bins $[15(l-1),15l)^\circ$, $l=1,\ldots,24$, with probability $p_l$, shape $\zeta=2$ and scale $\psi_l$ (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-wind}). The two wind data sets are:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Data Set~I}, nearly unidirectional, with the same wind-speed distribution in all directions: $\psi=13$~m/s in all bins, and 80\% of the probability in bins 6 and 7, i.e., wind from 165$^\circ$ to 195$^\circ$;
\item \emph{Data Set~II}, a broad rose with direction-dependent wind speeds: scales from 2.6 to 10~m/s and the highest probability in bin~13.
\end{itemize}
Both are synthetic wind roses of the Kusiak--Song benchmark~\cite{Kusiak2010,Bansal2017}, not measurements at a site; the measured wind climates in this study are the 12-sector Weibull roses of the Horns Rev~1 block (Section~\ref{sec:hornsrev-site}) and of a Lillgrund block (Section~\ref{sec:lillgrund}), both optimized in 5$^\circ$ bins, and IEA37 Case Study~1 uses 16 directions at one speed (Section~\ref{sec:setup-beyond}).
% verified: analysis/wflop_model.py (DS1_W, DS1_PSI, DS2_PSI, DS2_W; k = 2); bins 6+7 of DS I: 0.2+0.6 = 0.8, towards 75-105 deg = from 165-195 deg

A top-hat wake is narrow, so with 15$^\circ$ bins the wake of a distant pair of turbines can fall between two bin centers and not enter the objective; optimized layouts exploit these gaps (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-model-details}). Section~\ref{sec:direction} reports which conclusions survive a 1$^\circ$ rose.

\subsection{Wake Effect Model}
In the Jensen model (Fig.~\ref{fig:model}b,c), the wake of turbine $j$ is a half cone with half-angle $\alpha=\arctan K$ (wake-spreading constant $K$), and its virtual vertex lies $R/K$ upstream of the rotor. With the along-wind distance from the virtual vertex $u_{ij}=(x_i - x_j)\cos\theta + (y_i - y_j)\sin\theta + R/K$ and the lateral offset $v_{ij}=-(x_i - x_j)\sin\theta + (y_i - y_j)\cos\theta$, turbine $i$ lies in the wake if $\beta_{ij}=\cos^{-1}\!\bigl(u_{ij}/\sqrt{u_{ij}^2+v_{ij}^2}\bigr)<\alpha$. Its downstream distance is $d_{ij}=\lvert u_{ij}-R/K\rvert$, and with the thrust coefficient $C_T$ the velocity deficit, with the free-stream speed $s_{\text{up}}$ and the wake speed $s_{\text{down}}$, is
\begin{equation}
    \Delta_{ij} = 1-\frac{s_{\text{down}}}{s_{\text{up}}}=\frac{1 - \sqrt{1 - C_T}}{\left(1 + K d_{ij}/R\right)^2}.
    \label{eq:deficit}
\end{equation}
The numerator is the deficit behind the rotor from actuator-disc momentum theory, and the factor $R^2/(R+Kd_{ij})^2$ spreads it over the growing wake (Fig.~\ref{fig:model}b); with $C_T=0.8$ and $K=0.075$, a turbine $\ell_{\min}=308$~m directly behind another loses 21.6\% of the wind speed, one 770~m ($10D$) behind 8.8\%.
% verified: (1-sqrt(0.2))/(1+0.075*308/38.5)^2 = 0.2159; (1-sqrt(0.2))/(1+0.075*770/38.5)^2 = 0.0884
As in~\cite{Kusiak2010}, this top-hat wake is tested at the hub center only, so the deficit jumps at the cone edge, and it also reaches a turbine less than $R/K$ \emph{upstream} of $j$ near its axis (between $A$ and the rotor in Fig.~\ref{fig:model}c; non-physical, kept for comparability with the published results). The deficits are combined by the root-sum-square rule,
\begin{equation}\label{eq5}
\Delta_i(\theta) = \Bigl( \textstyle\sum_{j \ne i,\ \beta_{ij} < \alpha} \Delta_{ij}^2 \Bigr)^{1/2} .
\end{equation}
As $C_T$ is constant (which overstates the deficits above rated speed), $\Delta_i(\theta)$ depends only on the layout and the direction, not on the free-stream speed $s$; the local wind speed in a direction bin, $s_{\rm local}=[1-\Delta_i(\theta)]\,s$, is therefore a scaled Weibull variable, with the same shape $\zeta(\theta)$ and the scale $\psi_i(\theta)=\psi(\theta)[1-\Delta_i(\theta)]$. The hub-center test makes the objective jump where a hub crosses a cone edge in an evaluated direction; hence the derivative-free local search of VNS (Section~\ref{sec:vns}), and finite-difference gradients handicap MS-SLSQP.

The Jensen model is used for comparability with the published Kusiak--Song results; a Gaussian wake is tested in Sections~\ref{sec:iea37} and~\ref{sec:gaussian} (reasons: Section~\ref{S-sec:S-model-details}).

\subsection{Power Model and Expected Energy}
\label{sec:powermodel}
\label{sec:S-power}
The power curve of the Kusiak--Song benchmark~\cite{Kusiak2010} is
\begin{equation*}
f(s)=\begin{cases}
0, & s<s_{\text{cut-in}},\\
\lambda_1 s+\lambda_0, & s_{\text{cut-in}}\le s\le s_{\text{rated}},\\
P_{\text{rated}}, & s>s_{\text{rated}},
\end{cases}
\end{equation*}
with $s_{\text{cut-in}}=3.5$~m/s, $s_{\text{rated}}=14$~m/s, $P_{\text{rated}}=1500$~kW, slope $\lambda_1=140.86$ and intercept $\lambda_0=-500$. We use it as published (details: Section~\ref{S-sec:S-model-details}; a cubic curve is tested in Section~\ref{sec:powercurve}). The expected power of turbine $i$, whose power output $P_i=f(s_{\rm local})$ depends on its local wind speed,
\begin{equation}\label{eq8}
E(P_i) = \int_0^{360} p_\theta(\theta) \int_0^{\infty} f(s)\,
       p_s\bigl(s; \zeta(\theta), \psi_i(\theta)\bigr) \, ds \, d\theta ,
\end{equation}
is evaluated, as in~\cite{Kusiak2010}, by a Riemann sum over 24 direction bins of 15$^\circ$ and 21 speed bins of 0.5~m/s between $s_{\text{cut-in}}$ and $s_{\text{rated}}$ (Eq.~\eqref{S-eq:S-power-disc}).
Like the earlier studies, we keep the bin width ($15^\circ$) in degrees, so the benchmark objective $\sum_i E(P_i)$ is 15 times the expected farm power in kW, and the gross (wake-only) annual energy production is $\mathrm{AEP}\,[\mathrm{MWh}] = (\text{objective}/15)\times 8.76$. The wake-free benchmark objective of one turbine, $E_{\rm ideal}$, is the value of~\eqref{eq8} with $\psi_i=\psi$; for Data Set~I, $E_{\rm ideal}=14{,}045.74$, which corresponds to 936.38~kW (a capacity factor of 62\%, far above real sites) or 8,202.7~MWh/yr.
% verified: 14045.74/15 = 936.38 kW; 936.38/1500 = 0.624; 936.38*8.76 = 8202.7 MWh (reproduced from wflop_model.py); N_s = (14-3.5)/0.5 = 21 (wflop_model.expected_power_linear); 140.86*3.5-500 = -6.99; 500/140.86 = 3.55; 140.86*14-500 = 1472.0

\subsection{Optimization Problem and Constraint Handling}
\label{sec:constraints}
With the wake loss $L(\mathbf X)=N E_{\rm ideal}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}E(P_i)$, reported in percent of the wake-free value, $100\,L/(NE_{\rm ideal})$, the optimization problem reads
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\min_{\mathbf X\in[-r,r]^{2N}}\quad & L(\mathbf X)\\
\text{subject to}\quad & \ell_{ij}\ge\ell_{\min}, && 1\le i<j\le N \quad \text{(spacing)},\\
& x_i^2+y_i^2\le r^2, && 1\le i\le N \quad \text{(boundary)},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:wflop}
\end{equation}
which is equivalent to maximizing the expected farm power and hence the AEP: a problem in $2N$ variables ($2N\le30$ on the benchmark) with a discontinuous objective and $N(N-1)/2$ nonconvex spacing constraints.
After every position update, each coordinate is clipped to $[-r,r]$ (box repair; the square, not the circle). The constraints are handled by a penalty on the boundary and spacing violations
\begin{equation*}
g^{\rm b}_{i}=x_i^2+y_i^2-r^2,\qquad g^{\rm s}_{ij}=\ell_{\min}-\ell_{ij};
\end{equation*}
all optimizers except MS-SLSQP minimize the penalized wake loss
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
F_p(\mathbf X)={}&\Bigl(N E_{\rm ideal}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}E(P_i)\Bigr)+\sum_{i:\,g^{\rm b}_i>0}\bigl(1+\mu_{\rm p} g^{\rm b}_i\bigr)^2\\
&+\sum_{i<j:\,g^{\rm s}_{ij}>0}\bigl(1+\mu_{\rm p} g^{\rm s}_{ij}\bigr)^2,\qquad \mu_{\rm p}=10^{10},
\end{split}
\label{eq:penalty}
\end{equation}
whose first term is the wake loss $L(\mathbf X)$ reported in our tables. Three properties of this treatment matter for the comparisons.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Feasibility first.} Any violation above about $4.6\times10^{-8}$ (m or m$^2$) makes the penalty exceed any possible wake loss, so comparing $F_p$ values is, up to smaller violations, a feasibility-first rule (feasible before infeasible, then wake loss or total penalty).
\item \emph{Scaling of the violations.} The scaling ($g^{\rm b}_i$ in m$^2$, $g^{\rm s}_{ij}$ in m) favors infeasible layouts with turbines slightly too close, or with a turbine clipped to the box near a tangent point $(\pm r,y_i)$, where $g^{\rm b}_i=y_i^2$.
\item \emph{Feasibility of the final layouts.} MS-SLSQP minimizes the wake loss under explicit constraints. A final layout counts as feasible if $\ell_{ij}\ge\ell_{\min}$ and $\sqrt{x_i^2+y_i^2}\le r$ hold within $10^{-6}$~m (Section~\ref{sec:setup}), a looser tolerance than the penalty's. With random starts, no optimizer guarantees a feasible final layout; infeasible final layouts are therefore counted, reported and ranked below feasible ones (Section~\ref{sec:setup-stats}).
\end{itemize}
Section~\ref{sec:boundary} tests an alternative: Deb's feasibility rules~\cite{Deb2000} on dimensionless violations, with box clipping or radial projection.

% begin optA/sw/04_methods.tex
\section{Preliminaries: PSO, SSA and Basic VNS}
\label{sec:prelim}
% Proposition environment (neither IEEEtran nor elsarticle defines one); guarded, so a main file may define it in its preamble instead.
\makeatletter\@ifundefined{proposition}{\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}}{}\makeatother
A population method keeps $N_p$ candidate vectors $\mathbf X^i\in\mathbb R^{n}$ with components $X^i_m$ and bounds $lb_m=-r$, $ub_m=r$; unless stated otherwise, one iteration costs $N_p$ objective evaluations. This section states the methods exactly as implemented (Algorithms~\ref{alg:pso}--\ref{alg:vns}); Table~\ref{tab:params} collects all settings.

\subsection{Particle Swarm Optimization}
\label{sec:pso}
We use the standard PSO, proposed by Kennedy and Eberhart~\cite{Kennedy1995}, in the inertia-weight form~\cite{Shi1998} with the constriction coefficients of Clerc and Kennedy~\cite{Clerc2002} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:pso}), with a global best, run for $T$ iterations $t=1,\ldots,T$. Particle $i$ has a position $\mathbf X^i$, a velocity $\mathbf V^i$ and a personal best $\mathbf P^i$, the swarm keeps the global best $\mathbf G$, and a particle moves by
\begin{align}
\mathbf V^i&\leftarrow w\,\mathbf V^i+c_1\boldsymbol\rho_1\circ\bigl(\mathbf P^i-\mathbf X^i\bigr)+c_2\boldsymbol\rho_2\circ\bigl(\mathbf G-\mathbf X^i\bigr),\label{eq:pso-v}\\
\mathbf X^i&\leftarrow\mathbf X^i+\mathbf V^i,\label{eq:pso-x}
\end{align}
where $\boldsymbol\rho_1,\boldsymbol\rho_2$ are vectors of independent $U(0,1)$ numbers, drawn anew for every move, and $\circ$ is the componentwise product. The constriction PSO of Clerc and Kennedy~\cite{Clerc2002} with their standard choice $\varphi_1=\varphi_2=2.05$ (constriction factor $\kappa=0.7298$) is identical to~\eqref{eq:pso-v} with
\begin{equation}
w=0.7298,\qquad c_1=c_2=\kappa\varphi_1=1.49618 .
\label{eq:constriction}
\end{equation}
PSO denotes~\eqref{eq:pso-v} and~\eqref{eq:pso-x} with these coefficients, fixed a priori from the literature. It is run as implemented: velocities start at zero and are not clamped (no bound $\lvert V^i_m\rvert\le V_{\max}$), a coordinate clipped to the square (Section~\ref{sec:constraints}) keeps its velocity, and $\mathbf P^i$, $\mathbf G$ are updated after each evaluation (asynchronous global best).

\begin{algorithm}[!t]
\caption{PSO for the WFLOP as implemented (complete procedure; $N_p(T+1)$ evaluations of $F_p$).}
\label{alg:pso}
\small
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require site (farm radius $r$), number of turbines $N$, wind data and wake model, i.e., the penalized wake loss $F_p$~\eqref{eq:penalty}; number of iterations $T$; random seed
\Statex \textbf{Parameters:} swarm size $N_p=30$; $w=0.7298$, $c_1=c_2=1.49618$~\eqref{eq:constriction}
\Ensure the global best layout $\mathbf G$ and its value $F_p(\mathbf G)$
\Statex \textbf{Initialization}
\State $n\gets2N$; draw (seeded) $N_p$ layouts $\mathbf X^i\in[-r,r]^n$ and evaluate $F_p(\mathbf X^i)$ \Comment{$N_p$ evaluations}
\State $\mathbf V^i\gets\mathbf 0$, $\mathbf P^i\gets\mathbf X^i$ ($i=1,\ldots,N_p$); $\mathbf G\gets\arg\min_i F_p(\mathbf P^i)$
\Statex \textbf{Main loop}
\For{$t=1,\ldots,T$}
    \For{$i=1,\ldots,N_p$}
        \State Draw $\boldsymbol\rho_1,\boldsymbol\rho_2\sim U(0,1)^n$; $\mathbf V^i\gets w\,\mathbf V^i+c_1\boldsymbol\rho_1\circ(\mathbf P^i-\mathbf X^i)+c_2\boldsymbol\rho_2\circ(\mathbf G-\mathbf X^i)$ \Comment{\eqref{eq:pso-v}}
        \State $\mathbf X^i\gets\operatorname{clip}(\mathbf X^i+\mathbf V^i,-r,r)$ \Comment{\eqref{eq:pso-x} with box repair; $\mathbf V^i$ is kept}
        \State Evaluate $F_p(\mathbf X^i)$; if $F_p(\mathbf X^i)<F_p(\mathbf P^i)$, then $\mathbf P^i\gets\mathbf X^i$; if $F_p(\mathbf X^i)<F_p(\mathbf G)$, then $\mathbf G\gets\mathbf X^i$ \Comment{asynchronous update}
    \EndFor
\EndFor
\Statex \textbf{Termination}
\State Stop after $T$ iterations, i.e., $N_p(T+1)$ evaluations; \Return $\mathbf G$
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\emph{Stability.} If $\mathbf P^i$ and $\mathbf G$ stay fixed (stagnation) and no bound is active, every coordinate follows a random linear recursion; for $c_1=c_2$, its mean and variance converge if and only if $\lvert w\rvert<1$ and $c_1+c_2<24(1-w^2)/(7-5w)$, the order-2 condition of Poli~\cite{Poli2009,Bonyadi2017}. These known results, restated for the implemented update with their proof in Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-pso-stability}, are used only to check the setting, not claimed as new theory. The constriction setting satisfies the condition ($c_1+c_2=2.99<3.35$; Fig.~\ref{S-fig:S-th-stability}; Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-constriction}): in the limit, a coordinate with $P^i_m\ne G_m$ is sampled with mean $(P^i_m+G_m)/2$ and standard deviation $1.04\,\lvert P^i_m-G_m\rvert$, so the swarm keeps sampling around $\mathbf G$ unless $\mathbf P^i=\mathbf G$. Stability does not say how good $\mathbf G$ is, and clipping at the bounds breaks the second assumption (Lemma~\ref{S-lem:S-clip}).
% CHECK-THEORY [T02]-[T06], [T12]: stability summary (full statement and proof: S-prop:S-pso-stability in the supplement; order-1 via E[phi]=(c1+c2)/2; Poli's bound exact for c1=c2, analytic region = numerical region, T06); 24(1-0.7298^2)/(7-5*0.7298)=3.348; 2*1.49618=2.99; spectral radius of the second-moment matrix 0.944 (constriction) in Table S-th-settings
% CHECK-THEORY [T05]: SD factor 1.04 = sqrt(V_inf/(p-g)^2) = sqrt(1.0874) (constriction); Lemma S-lem:S-clip (b): clipped coordinate stays at the bound while v' >= 0
The scale factor $F=0.5$ of differential evolution (DE; Section~\ref{sec:setup-methods}) exceeds Zaharie's critical value (0.14 at $\mathrm{CR}=0.9$)~\cite{Zaharie2002}, so its weak results are not due to premature variance loss.
% verified (Zaharie): critical F = sqrt((1-CR/2)/N_p) = sqrt((1-0.45)/30)=0.135 (N_p = 30, CR = 0.9, F = 0.5: Section setup); F above the critical value = no variance collapse; Cleghorn2018 cited for c1 = c2 only

\subsection{Salp Swarm Algorithm and Its Laplacian Variant}
\label{sec:ssa}
\label{sec:S-ssa}
The SSA was proposed by Mirjalili et al.~\cite{Mirjalili2017} (pseudocode: Algorithm~\ref{S-alg:S-ssa}). Its population of $N_p$ salps, sorted by $F_p$, consists of two halves:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Food source.} The best solution found so far, i.e., the candidate with the best fitness (lowest $F_p$), is the food source $\mathbf H$; after every iteration, the best salp of the sorted population, the first leader, replaces $\mathbf H$ if it is better.
\item \emph{Leaders.} The better half, $i\le N_p/2$, updates each coordinate around the food source by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:leader}
X_m^i = H_m\pm\xi_1\big((ub_m-lb_m)\xi_2+lb_m\big),
\end{equation}
with either sign with probability $1/2$, $\xi_2\sim U(0,1)$ and $\xi_1=2\exp[-(4t/T)^2]$ decreasing with the iteration $t$ out of $T$: a leader samples $H_m+U(-\xi_1r,\xi_1r)$ in every coordinate, with $\xi_1\le0.037$ for $t\ge T/2$.
\item \emph{Followers.} A follower $i>N_p/2$ moves to the midpoint of its position and the \emph{new} position of the preceding salp.
\end{itemize}
The leaders thus perturb $\mathbf H$ uniformly with a shrinking radius, and the followers only average~\cite{Castelli2022}. The SSA has known drawbacks, such as premature convergence and an imbalance between exploration and exploitation~\cite{Castelli2022,Solanki2023}. Solanki and Deep~\cite{Solanki2023} addressed them by proposing a new variant, the Laplacian SSA (LX-SSA). LX-SSA gives each follower a second candidate $Y_m^i=X_m^i+\gamma(H_m-X_m^i)$ with one Laplace step~\cite{Deep2007} for all coordinates,
\begin{equation}
\gamma=\phi-\chi\,\mathrm{sgn}\bigl(z-\tfrac12\bigr)\ln\bigl(1-2\lvert z-\tfrac12\rvert\bigr),\quad z\sim U(0,1),
\label{eq:laplace}
\end{equation}
with the published, untuned values $\phi=0$ (centered on $\mathbf X^i$) and $\chi=1$ (mean step length equal to the distance to $\mathbf H$), and keeps the better candidate. As published, it evaluates both candidates of each follower and then re-evaluates the kept ones with the new population, so an iteration costs $2N_p$ evaluations (SSA: $N_p$) and LX-SSA runs half as many iterations as SSA (Table~\ref{tab:params}). Comparing the two tests LX-SSA as published, not the Laplace step alone (for the step itself, see the ablation in Section~\ref{sec:abl-swarm}); both use the code of the published LX-SSA study~\cite{Solanki2023}.
% CHECK-THEORY [T11]: SSA xi_1(T/2) = 2 exp(-4) = 0.0366 ("xi_1 <= 0.037 for t >= T/2"); leader step xi_1((ub-lb) xi_2 + lb) = xi_1 r (2 xi_2 - 1) (S-th-feas note)
% verified: analysis/authors_optimizers.py SSA/LXSSA: leaders = pop_size // 2 = 15; follower = (pop[i] + new_pop[i-1])/2; LX: one scalar gamma per follower, candidate = pop[i] + gamma (food - pop[i]); both clipped and evaluated, then whole population evaluated (2 N_p per iteration)

%         \State LX: form $\mathbf Y^i$ by \eqref{eq:laplace}, box-repair $\mathbf Y^i$ and $\mathbf Z^i$, evaluate both, $\tilde{\mathbf X}^i\gets$ the better \Comment{[2 per follower]}
%     \EndFor
%     \State Box-repair $\tilde{\mathbf X}$, set $\mathbf X\gets\tilde{\mathbf X}$, evaluate $F_p$ for all salps, sort them, and update $\mathbf H$. \Comment{[$N_p$]}
% \EndFor
% \State \Return $\mathbf H$.
% \end{algorithmic}
% \end{algorithm}

\subsection{Basic Variable Neighborhood Search}
\label{sec:vns}
The basic VNS~\cite{Mladenovic1997,Hansen2001}, in the continuous form of~\cite{Mladenovic2008} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:vns}), improves one incumbent $\mathbf X$ with $k_{\max}=5$ $\ell_\infty$ shell neighborhoods $\mathcal N_k(\mathbf X)=\{\mathbf X':\delta_{k-1}<\lVert \mathbf X'-\mathbf X\rVert_\infty\le\delta_k\}$, $\delta_k=0.1\,k\,r$ ($\delta_0=0$), of the whole layout: shaking draws a uniform point of $\mathcal N_k(\mathbf X)$, clipped to the box, and a best-improvement local search improves it. As the top-hat wake makes $F_p$ discontinuous, the local search is a compass search: it evaluates all $2n$ moves $\pm h\,\mathbf e_m$ ($\mathbf e_m$ the $m$th unit vector; 60 evaluations for $N=15$), moves to the best one if it strictly improves, and halves $h$ (from $h_0=0.05r$) when none does, until $h<h_{\min}=10^{-3}r$. A completed descent ends at a point that no move $\pm\bar h\,\mathbf e_m$, $\bar h=h_0/2^5=0.0015625\,r$, improves (Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-ls}), a coordinate-wise optimality at one step length, not a local minimum.
% CHECK-THEORY [T12]: compass search final step h0/2^5 = 0.0015625 r (Proposition S-prop:S-ls); verified: analysis/original_vns.py (k_max = 5, rho_k = 0.1 k r, h0 = 0.05 r, h_min = 1e-3 r; move to the best of the 2 dim moves if it improves the current point, else halve h; shaking by rejection in the l_inf shell, then clip)

\begin{algorithm}[!htb]
\caption{Basic VNS for the WFLOP as implemented (complete procedure; every call of $F_p$ counts against the budget $B$).}
\label{alg:vns}
\small
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require site (farm radius $r$), number of turbines $N$, wind data and wake model, i.e., the penalized wake loss $F_p$~\eqref{eq:penalty}; budget $B$; random seed; an incumbent layout $\mathbf X$ (VNS alone: none; Phase~2 of a hybrid: the layout passed by Phase~1)
\Statex \textbf{Parameters:} $k_{\max}=5$, $\delta_k=0.1\,k\,r$ ($\delta_0=0$); $h_0=0.05\,r$, $h_{\min}=10^{-3}r$
\Ensure the best layout $\mathbf X^\star$ evaluated
\Statex \textbf{Initialization}
\State $n\gets2N$; \textbf{if} no incumbent is given \textbf{then} draw (seeded) $N_p=30$ layouts in $[-r,r]^n$, evaluate them and let $\mathbf X$ be the best \Comment{VNS alone}
\State $\mathbf X\gets\textsc{LocalSearch}(\mathbf X)$; $k\gets1$
\Statex \textbf{Main loop}
\While{fewer than $B$ evaluations have been used}
    \State Draw $\mathbf d\sim U[-\delta_k,\delta_k]^n$ until $\lVert\mathbf d\rVert_\infty>\delta_{k-1}$ \Comment{shaking in $\mathcal N_k(\mathbf X)$}
    \State $\mathbf X'\gets\operatorname{clip}(\mathbf X+\mathbf d,-r,r)$; evaluate $F_p(\mathbf X')$
    \State $\mathbf X''\gets\textsc{LocalSearch}(\mathbf X')$
    \If{$F_p(\mathbf X'')<F_p(\mathbf X)$} $\mathbf X\gets\mathbf X''$, $k\gets1$ \Comment{move}
    \Else\ $k\gets k\bmod k_{\max}+1$ \Comment{next, larger neighborhood}
    \EndIf
\EndWhile
\Statex \textbf{Termination}
\State Stop as soon as $B$ evaluations have been used (also inside \textsc{LocalSearch}); \Return $\mathbf X^\star$, the best layout evaluated
\Statex
\Function{LocalSearch}{$\mathbf y$} \Comment{compass search, best improvement}
    \State $h\gets h_0$
    \While{$h\ge h_{\min}$}
        \State Evaluate the $2n$ trial layouts $\operatorname{clip}(\mathbf y\pm h\,\mathbf e_m,-r,r)$, $m=1,\ldots,n$ ($\mathbf e_m$: $m$th unit vector)
        \If{the best trial layout $\mathbf y'$ has $F_p(\mathbf y')<F_p(\mathbf y)$} $\mathbf y\gets\mathbf y'$ \Else\ $h\gets h/2$
        \EndIf
    \EndWhile
    \State \Return $\mathbf y$
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\section{The Proposed PSO-VNS}
\label{sec:psovns-method}
This section motivates PSO-VNS, states the algorithm and gives its properties. PSO-VNS combines the PSO of Algorithm~\ref{alg:pso}, in the constriction setting~\eqref{eq:constriction}, with the basic VNS of Algorithm~\ref{alg:vns} in two consecutive phases.

\subsection{Motivation}
\label{sec:psovns-motivation}
Three properties of the continuous WFLOP shape the design.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Feasible layouts are hard to reach by sampling.} The feasible region is a small, nonconvex part of the bounding box; for the largest benchmark cases, fewer than one uniform layout in $10^6$ is feasible (Monte Carlo estimate for $N=12$ and 15; Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-box} and Table~\ref{S-tab:S-th-box}). A method that refines a single layout therefore needs a good, preferably feasible, start, and a population-based global search that moves whole layouts towards the feasible region is a natural source of such starts. PSO is widely used for the WFLOP~\cite{Pookpunt2013,Asaah2021}, has few parameters, and its stability under stagnation is understood~\cite{Poli2009,Bonyadi2017,Cleghorn2018}, so that a setting can be checked before it is used (Section~\ref{sec:pso}).
\item \emph{PSO is a weak finisher.} Under a stable setting and while the best positions stay fixed, the expected position of a particle converges to a weighted average of its personal best and the global best~\cite{Trelea2003,VandenBergh2006}, a point that need not be a local optimum~\cite{Bonyadi2017}; late in a run, refinement thus relies on random sampling around the global best. The last improvements of a layout, however, are small moves of individual turbine coordinates, which a coordinate-wise local search makes systematically.
\item \emph{The objective is discontinuous.} With the top-hat wake, $F_p$ jumps where a turbine enters or leaves a wake cone (Section~\ref{sec:model}), so gradient-based refinement is unreliable and a derivative-free local search is needed. The basic VNS~\cite{Mladenovic1997,Hansen2001}, in its continuous form~\cite{Mladenovic2008}, fits this need: its local search, a compass search, compares objective values only; its shaking in nested neighborhoods of the whole layout gives a systematic way to leave a local optimum (at the 6,030-evaluation budget studied here it rarely acts; Section~\ref{sec:abl-vns}); and its few parameters can be kept at their defaults.
\end{itemize}

\emph{The need for a new variant.} PSO and VNS have been combined before, with VNS as the local search applied within the PSO loop~\cite{Tasgetiren2007,Marinakis2017,Gumaida2019} (Section~\ref{sec:related-hybrid}). Such embedded designs spend local-search evaluations in every iteration, which makes the cost of each component hard to separate, and, to our knowledge, they have not been applied to the WFLOP. PSO-VNS instead runs the two methods in sequence: PSO for a share $\omega$ of the budget, then VNS from the best layout found. This design
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] uses the swarm where it is strong, in reaching the feasible region from random starts, and the local search where the swarm is weak, in refining one layout;
\item[(ii)] gives both phases fixed, exactly counted budgets;
\item[(iii)] adds a single parameter, $\omega$, to those of PSO and VNS; and
\item[(iv)] keeps Phase~1 a plain PSO, so that the contribution of the local-search phase can be measured directly against PSO with the same seeds (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-props}).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Algorithm}
\label{sec:psovns-alg}
PSO-VNS (Algorithm~\ref{alg:hybrid}) is a new two-phase variant of the PSO--VNS hybrid. \emph{Phase~1} runs the PSO of Algorithm~\ref{alg:pso} from the seeded initial population for $T_1=\operatorname{round}((\omega B-N_p)/N_p)$ iterations, that is, $N_p(T_1+1)$ evaluations, and passes its global best layout $\mathbf G$ to Phase~2, feasible or not; the penalty then guides the local search towards feasibility. \emph{Phase~2} applies the basic VNS (Algorithm~\ref{alg:vns}) to $\mathbf G$: a compass-search descent, then, until the budget $B$ is used, shaking in the neighborhood $\mathcal N_k$ followed by a descent, accepting an improvement and returning to $k=1$, or else moving to the next, larger neighborhood. The best layout evaluated is returned.

\begin{algorithm}[!t]
\caption{PSO-VNS for the WFLOP (complete procedure; every call of $F_p$ counts against the budget $B$).}
\label{alg:hybrid}
\small
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require site (farm radius $r$), number of turbines $N$, wind data and wake model, i.e., the penalized wake loss $F_p$~\eqref{eq:penalty}; budget $B$; random seed
\Statex \textbf{Parameters:} population $N_p=30$; $w=0.7298$, $c_1=c_2=1.49618$~\eqref{eq:constriction}; split $\omega=0.5$; $k_{\max}=5$, $\delta_k=0.1\,k\,r$ ($\delta_0=0$); $h_0=0.05\,r$, $h_{\min}=10^{-3}r$
\Ensure the best layout $\mathbf X^\star$ evaluated, with its wake loss and feasibility
\Statex \textbf{Initialization}
\State $n\gets2N$; $T_1\gets\operatorname{round}((\omega B-N_p)/N_p)$ \Comment{$T_1=100$ for $B=6{,}030$}
\State Draw (seeded) $N_p$ layouts $\mathbf X^i\in[-r,r]^n$ and evaluate $F_p(\mathbf X^i)$ \Comment{$N_p$ evaluations}
\State $\mathbf V^i\gets\mathbf 0$, $\mathbf P^i\gets\mathbf X^i$ ($i=1,\ldots,N_p$); $\mathbf G\gets\arg\min_i F_p(\mathbf P^i)$
\Statex \textbf{Phase 1: PSO} (global search towards the feasible region)
\For{$t=1,\ldots,T_1$}
    \For{$i=1,\ldots,N_p$}
        \State Draw $\boldsymbol\rho_1,\boldsymbol\rho_2\sim U(0,1)^n$; $\mathbf V^i\gets w\,\mathbf V^i+c_1\boldsymbol\rho_1\circ(\mathbf P^i-\mathbf X^i)+c_2\boldsymbol\rho_2\circ(\mathbf G-\mathbf X^i)$
        \State $\mathbf X^i\gets\operatorname{clip}(\mathbf X^i+\mathbf V^i,-r,r)$ \Comment{box repair; $\mathbf V^i$ is kept}
        \State Evaluate $F_p(\mathbf X^i)$; if $F_p(\mathbf X^i)<F_p(\mathbf P^i)$, then $\mathbf P^i\gets\mathbf X^i$; if $F_p(\mathbf X^i)<F_p(\mathbf G)$, then $\mathbf G\gets\mathbf X^i$
    \EndFor
\EndFor \Comment{$N_p(T_1+1)$ evaluations used}
\Statex \textbf{Phase 2: basic VNS} (local refinement of $\mathbf G$)
\State $\mathbf X\gets\textsc{LocalSearch}(\mathbf G)$; $k\gets1$
\While{fewer than $B$ evaluations have been used}
    \State Draw $\mathbf d\sim U[-\delta_k,\delta_k]^n$ until $\lVert\mathbf d\rVert_\infty>\delta_{k-1}$ \Comment{shaking in $\mathcal N_k(\mathbf X)$}
    \State $\mathbf X'\gets\operatorname{clip}(\mathbf X+\mathbf d,-r,r)$; evaluate $F_p(\mathbf X')$
    \State $\mathbf X''\gets\textsc{LocalSearch}(\mathbf X')$
    \If{$F_p(\mathbf X'')<F_p(\mathbf X)$} $\mathbf X\gets\mathbf X''$, $k\gets1$ \Comment{move}
    \Else\ $k\gets k\bmod k_{\max}+1$ \Comment{next, larger neighborhood}
    \EndIf
\EndWhile
\Statex \textbf{Termination}
\State Stop as soon as $B$ evaluations have been used (also inside \textsc{LocalSearch}); \Return $\mathbf X^\star$, the best layout evaluated in either phase
\Statex
\Function{LocalSearch}{$\mathbf y$} \Comment{compass search, best improvement}
    \State $h\gets h_0$
    \While{$h\ge h_{\min}$}
        \State Evaluate the $2n$ trial layouts $\operatorname{clip}(\mathbf y\pm h\,\mathbf e_m,-r,r)$, $m=1,\ldots,n$ ($\mathbf e_m$: $m$th unit vector)
        \If{the best trial layout $\mathbf y'$ has $F_p(\mathbf y')<F_p(\mathbf y)$} $\mathbf y\gets\mathbf y'$ \Else\ $h\gets h/2$
        \EndIf
    \EndWhile
    \State \Return $\mathbf y$
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

With $\omega=0.5$ and $B=6{,}030$, PSO runs for $T_1=100$ iterations (99.5 rounded half to even) and VNS receives 3,000 evaluations (Table~\ref{tab:params}). None of these values was tuned; Section~\ref{sec:split} studies $\omega$. At this budget, PSO-VNS is essentially PSO plus one compass-search descent, and the neighborhoods matter only for small $N$ (Section~\ref{sec:abl-vns}).
% CHECK-DIAG [D08]: essentially one compass-search descent (median shaking steps \NDShakesPSOVNS, first descent \NDDescentSharePSOVNS % of the Phase-2 improvement; 12 split cases x 10 seeds; same pattern for SSA-VNS, RS-VNS); [D20]: cycles \NDCycleEvalsPctPSOVNS % of the Phase-2 evaluations pooled (median run \NDCycleEvalsPctMedianPSOVNS %), accepted cycles \NDCycleSharePSOVNS % of the gain (\NDCycleAllSharePSOVNS % incl. the cycle cut off by the budget)
% CHECK-DIAG [D09]: N >= 12: first descent unfinished in \NDDescentUnfinishedLargePSOVNS of \NDRunsLargePSOVNS runs, median run without shaking ("neighborhoods matter only for small N")
% verified: Python round(99.5) = 100 (half to even); omega = 0.25/0.75/0.9 give 49/150/180

\subsection{Properties and Computational Cost}
\label{sec:psovns-props}
\emph{Exact prefix.} As the PSO coefficients do not depend on the iteration, Phase~1 reproduces exactly, for a given seed, the first $N_p(T_1+1)$ evaluations of a PSO run (Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-prefix}); comparing PSO-VNS with PSO thus isolates the effect of Phase~2. This does not hold for SSA-VNS and LX-SSA-VNS, whose $\xi_1$ depends on $t/T_1$ (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid}).

\emph{Cost.} A run uses exactly $B$ evaluations of $F_p$, including the initial population, and no gradients; the overhead of both phases is $\mathcal O(n)$ arithmetic per evaluation and $\mathcal O(N_pn)$ memory, small against a wake-model evaluation, so the run time is governed by $B$ (Section~\ref{sec:runtime}).

\emph{Constraints.} Neither phase enforces the constraints: both minimize the penalized loss $F_p$~\eqref{eq:penalty} over the box $[-r,r]^{2N}$, with clipping, and the feasibility of the returned layout is checked and reported for every run (Section~\ref{sec:setup}).

\section{Comparison Hybrids and Random-Sampling Controls}
\label{sec:hybrid}

\subsection{Two-Phase Template}
\label{sec:template}
The other hybrids and the random-sampling controls follow the sequential (relay) template of PSO-VNS (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-method}), so that they differ from it only in Phase~1. For a budget of $B$ evaluations and a split $\omega\in(0,1)$, Phase~1 starts from the seeded initial population, runs a population method for about $\omega B$ evaluations and passes its best layout to Phase~2, the basic VNS (Algorithm~\ref{alg:vns}), which applies the local search to it and then iterates until $B$ evaluations are used. Only Phase~1 differs:
\begin{itemize}
\item PSO in \emph{PSO-VNS};
\item SSA or LX-SSA in \emph{SSA-VNS} and \emph{LX-SSA-VNS} (with $\xi_1$ compressed to the Phase-1 iterations); and,
\item in the controls, $\operatorname{round}(\omega B)$ layouts: the initial population, then layouts drawn uniformly in the farm disc (\emph{RSD-VNS}) or in its bounding square (\emph{RS-VNS}).
\end{itemize}
VNS alone is the limiting case in which Phase~1 is the initial population. Phase~1 runs for
\begin{equation*}
T_1=\operatorname{round}((\omega B-N_p)/c_{\rm it})
\end{equation*}
iterations of $c_{\rm it}$ evaluations ($c_{\rm it}=N_p$ for PSO and SSA, $2N_p$ for LX-SSA).

The budget is not shared between methods: every run has its own $B$ evaluations, split in the same way between the two phases ($\omega=0.5$; 3,030 Phase-1 evaluations for the swarms, 3,015 for the controls). The hybrids and controls thus differ only in how Phase~1 spends its half, and a swarm phase is useful only if it gives VNS a better start than random sampling; the ablation (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}) tests this against RSD-VNS, with RS-VNS as a weak minimum bar.

Random layouts are rarely feasible: for the largest cases ($N=12$, 15), a layout drawn uniformly in the bounding square is feasible with probability below $10^{-6}$ (Monte Carlo; Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-box} and Table~\ref{S-tab:S-th-box}), so the 3,015 samples of RS-VNS contain about $10^{-3}$ or fewer feasible layouts in expectation, and RS-VNS hands VNS the least-violating sample. The spacing dominates: 730 RS-VNS runs have no feasible sample, and disc sampling gives one in only 242 of them.
% CHECK-DIAG [D17]: RS-VNS Phase-1 replay agrees with the runs and with (pi/4)^N; \NDRsRunsNoFeasSample (= \NRSRunsNoFeasSample) runs without any feasible Phase-1 sample
% CHECK-FINAL [C60]: spacing binding (R3-6): of the \NRSRunsNoFeasSample RS-VNS runs without a feasible sample, disc sampling (RSD-VNS, same runs) rescues \NRsSquareExplains < \NRsSpacingDominates that stay without one ("mainly because of the spacing"); [C61]: controls switch at 3,015, swarms at 3,030; RSD-VNS definition: mpce_experiments.RSDVNS (first 30 = common initial population, then polar-uniform disc samples)

Table~\ref{tab:params} lists the settings of all methods, the published defaults~\cite{Clerc2002,Mirjalili2017,Solanki2023,Storn1997,Mladenovic2008}; MS-SLSQP (SciPy's SLSQP~\cite{Kraft1988}) minimizes $L$ under the scaled constraints of~\eqref{eq:wflop} (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-slsqp}). At 30,030 and 120,030 evaluations (Section~\ref{sec:budget}), $T$ and $T_1$ follow the same rules.

Every run of every method uses exactly $B$ evaluations; the numbers of iterations differ because one iteration costs $N_p=30$ evaluations ($2N_p=60$ for LX-SSA), and a hybrid gives each of its two phases about half of $B$. \section{Experimental Setup}
\label{sec:setup}

\subsection{Wind Farm Types}
\label{sec:setup-cases}
\label{sec:setup-beyond}
The methods are compared on four wind farm types (Table~\ref{tab:farm-types}):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Type~I}, the Kusiak--Song benchmark~\cite{Kusiak2010,Bansal2017}: 68 cases of circular farms with radii of 500, 750 and 1000~m and 2--15 turbines, under two synthetic wind roses, Data Sets~I and~II (Section~\ref{sec:wind}; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-wind});
\item \emph{Type~II}, a 16-turbine block of the offshore farm Horns Rev~1 with its measured 12-sector Weibull wind climate;
\item \emph{Type~III}, a 16-turbine ($4\times4$) block of the offshore farm Lillgrund with its measured 12-sector Weibull wind climate;
\item \emph{Type~IV}, IEA37 Case Study~1~\cite{Baker2019,IEA37repo}: circular farms with 16 and 36 turbines and a 16-direction wind rose with one wind speed; it tests optimizers rather than represents a site.
\end{enumerate}

Further studies on the six largest Type~I cases and on the Type~II block (Table~\ref{tab:design}) also use 30,030 and 120,030 evaluations (10 seeds for Type~II at 120,030) and a feasibility-preserving initialization, which draws seeded layouts inside the site and separates the turbines with L-BFGS-B on a packing penalty, without objective evaluations (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-feasinit-impl}).

\subsection{Compared Methods}
\label{sec:setup-methods}
The main comparison runs the same nine methods on every wind farm type; Table~\ref{tab:params} (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid}) lists their settings and budgets ($N_p=30$ for the population methods):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{PSO-VNS}, the proposed two-phase method (Algorithm~\ref{alg:hybrid}, $\omega=0.5$);
\item \emph{PSO}, the standard PSO of Kennedy and Eberhart~\cite{Kennedy1995} with the constriction coefficients of Clerc and Kennedy~\cite{Clerc2002};
\item \emph{SSA-VNS}, the two-phase template of PSO-VNS with SSA in Phase~1 (Section~\ref{sec:template});
\item \emph{SSA}, the salp swarm algorithm~\cite{Mirjalili2017};
\item \emph{LX-SSA}, the Laplacian SSA~\cite{Solanki2023};
\item \emph{DE}, DE/rand/1/bin~\cite{Storn1997};
\item \emph{GA}, a standard real-coded genetic algorithm (binary tournament, simulated binary crossover, polynomial mutation, elitism; settings in Table~\ref{tab:params}, untuned);
\item \emph{VNS}, the basic VNS alone (Algorithm~\ref{alg:vns});
\item \emph{MS-SLSQP}, multistart sequential least-squares programming (SciPy's SLSQP~\cite{Kraft1988}, forward-difference gradients), which minimizes the wake loss under explicit constraints and restarts from the initial, then from random layouts (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-slsqp}).
\end{enumerate}
All methods except MS-SLSQP minimize $F_p$~\eqref{eq:penalty} (Type~I: over $[-r,r]^{2N}$). GA uses the same protocol, seeds and budgets as the other methods on all four types (Table~\ref{tab:design}). Grid-based GAs~\cite{Mosetti1994,Grady2005} optimize a discrete formulation, and ACO~\cite{Eroglu2012} and BBO~\cite{Bansal2017} were not re-implemented; the pool is a controlled set of baselines, not a survey of WFLOP optimizers (Section~\ref{sec:limits}).

The \emph{ablation study} of PSO-VNS (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}) keeps its two-phase template and changes only Phase~1 (Section~\ref{sec:template}): SSA-VNS and \emph{LX-SSA-VNS} use SSA or LX-SSA, and the random-sampling controls \emph{RS-VNS} and \emph{RSD-VNS} draw, after the common initial population, uniform layouts in the bounding square or in the farm disc (3,015 Phase-1 evaluations versus the hybrids' 3,030). A split study varies $\omega$ on twelve cases (Section~\ref{sec:split}). Table~\ref{tab:design} lists all studies; the re-evaluations and diagnostics use stored layouts and runs.
