\subsection{Comparison Design and Statistics}
\label{sec:setup-stats}
All comparisons follow the rules below. The scoring of runs, the rounding and the Holm families are detailed in Section~\ref{S-sec:S-stats}, and the equivalence analysis in Section~\ref{S-sec:S-th-equiv}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Equal budgets.} Every run uses exactly the budget $B$ of its study ($B=6{,}030$ objective evaluations unless stated otherwise). The budget counts every call of the objective: the initial population, repeated evaluations of a layout and the finite-difference calls of MS-SLSQP; an exact gradient (IEA37 only) is charged as $c_g=3$ evaluations (Section~\ref{sec:iea37}). Work outside the objective is not counted: the optimizer's own arithmetic, the constraint checks, the internal subproblems of SLSQP and the geometric packing of the feasibility-preserving initialization.
\item \emph{Equal budgets do not mean equal elapsed times.} Two methods with the same budget make the same number of objective calls, but the uncounted work differs between them (MS-SLSQP, for example, also solves the SLSQP subproblems and needs more than twice as long per evaluation as PSO), and the elapsed time also depends on the machine and its load. Elapsed times are therefore reported separately (Section~\ref{sec:runtime}).
\item \emph{Paired seeds.} Every method uses seeds 1--30 and draws its initial population first, so runs with the same seed start from the same population and are paired.
% verified 2026-09-28: first Curve value identical across fresh_grid/vgrid/bgrid and mpce_psoc/psobv/rsvns/slsqp (13 files) for all 792 case-seed pairs with a feasible initial best (else NaN)
\item \emph{Feasibility.} A final layout is feasible if it meets the spacing and boundary constraints within $10^{-6}$~m (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-tol}). Method $a$ is \emph{qualified} in case $c$ if at least half of its runs are feasible (15 of 30; 5 of 10 with 10 seeds); $\bar L_{a,c}$ is the mean wake loss of its feasible runs. For two methods $A$ and $B$, $d_c=\bar L_{A,c}-\bar L_{B,c}$ is the case-mean difference (percentage points, pp; negative: $A$ better) and $\overline{\Delta L}$ the mean of $d_c$ over the cases in which both are qualified.
\item \emph{Ranking.} Within a case, the qualified methods are ranked by the mean benchmark objective of their feasible runs (rounded to $10^{-6}$; ties share their average rank), and the other methods below them by their number of feasible runs. The rank thus measures solution quality above a success threshold, not reliability (15 of 30 feasible runs can outrank 30 of 30); reliability is reported separately, by the feasible-run counts and an all-run paired outcome (Section~\ref{sec:archive-reliability}).
\item \emph{Tests.} All tests are two-sided ($\alpha=0.05$); Holm's correction~\cite{Holm1979} is applied over the comparisons of one table.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Friedman test on the case ranks (with the Iman--Davenport statistic) and post hoc $z$ tests of each method's average rank against that of PSO-VNS, $z=(\bar R_a-\bar R_{\text{PSO-VNS}})/\sqrt{k(k+1)/(6n)}$ for $k$ methods and $n$ cases~\cite{Demsar2006,Derrac2011,LaTorre2021}.
\item[(b)] Holm-adjusted Wilcoxon signed-rank test on the case means ($d_c$), the primary test for differences~\cite{Benavoli2016}; a case in which only one method is qualified counts as a maximal difference in its favor (the test then concerns a combined score of wake loss and qualification), and one in which neither is, is dropped. $\overline{\Delta L}$ is given with a 95\% percentile bootstrap CI (10{,}000 resamples of the cases) and, for key pairs, with the Hodges--Lehmann estimate (Table~\ref{S-tab:X-hl}).
\item[(c)] Per case, Wilcoxon signed-rank test on the 30 seed-paired runs, scored feasibility first, with the rank-biserial correlation $r_{\rm rb}$; W/T/L counts the cases with a significant win, no significant difference and a significant loss.
\item[(d)] Exact McNemar test on the seed-paired feasibility outcomes (feasible or not) of two methods in one case.
\item[(e)] Practical equivalence by two one-sided tests (TOST)~\cite{Lakens2017} at the margin $m=0.05$~pp, decided by interval inclusion (Definition~\ref{def:S-equiv}), and the Bayesian signed-rank test~\cite{Benavoli2017}, which reports $P_{\rm rope}$ ($P_{\rm A}$, $P_{\rm B}$), the posterior probability that practical equivalence ($A$ better, $B$ better by more than $m$) is the most probable of the three outcomes (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-th-equiv}).
\end{enumerate}
% CHECK (manual, 2026-09-29): constants in analysis/mpce_results.py: BOOT_N = 10000 (fixed seed), wil() drops |d| <= 1e-9, rank_rule rounds to 1e-6 and ranks non-qualified methods by nfeas (average ranks for ties); Holm families: friedman_block (vs focus), case_mean_wilcoxon (per table), paired_vs / ablation contrasts (per case and table)
\item \emph{Inference hierarchy.}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] Differences rest on the case-mean Wilcoxon tests~(b); the 68 cases are designed, not sampled, so their $p$ values describe this benchmark, not a population of farms.
\item[(ii)] Two equivalence questions are primary, each assessed on its own at $\alpha=0.05$ and decided at the seed level (Definition~\ref{def:S-equiv}(a)): whether the VNS phase adds a practically relevant gain to PSO (PSO-VNS vs.\ PSO, two-sided equivalence), and whether an SSA first phase gives a practically relevant gain over disc sampling (SSA-VNS vs.\ RSD-VNS, non-superiority). The case and cluster levels are sensitivity analyses; all other equivalence results are exploratory.
\item[(iii)] Average ranks with their Friedman and post hoc tests, and the W/T/L counts, are descriptive summaries; the ranks depend on the method pool.
\item[(iv)] The all-run paired outcome is a secondary endpoint: it counts wins and does not measure the size of a difference.
\item[(v)] The additional experiments and sensitivity studies of Table~\ref{tab:design} are secondary analyses.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{definition}[Practical equivalence at three levels of inference]
\label{def:S-equiv}
Let $A$ and $B$ be two methods, $d_c$ their case-mean difference over the $n$ cases in which both are qualified, $\overline{\Delta L}=\frac1n\sum_c d_c$, and $m>0$ a margin (not to be confused with the coordinate index $m$ of Section~\ref{sec:prelim}). $A$ and $B$ are \emph{practically equivalent at margin $m$} at a given level if both one-sided hypotheses $H_0^-\colon\Delta\le-m$ and $H_0^+\colon\Delta\ge m$ about the estimand $\Delta$ of that level are rejected at $\alpha=0.05$, i.e., if the 90\% interval for $\Delta$ lies inside $(-m,m)$. The three levels are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] \emph{Seed level} (the fixed benchmark). Estimand: the benchmark average of the expected case-mean differences; only the run-to-run variability is random. Interval: percentile bootstrap that resamples the 30 seed-paired runs within every case (the same seeds for both methods; case means over the feasible resampled runs).
\item[(b)] \emph{Case level} (exchangeability sensitivity). Estimand: the mean case difference under a hypothetical exchangeable distribution represented by the 68 designed cases. Interval: percentile bootstrap over cases, treating them as independent; a sensitivity analysis, as the cases are a designed benchmark, not a random sample of farms.
\item[(c)] \emph{Cluster level} (benchmark-group sensitivity). Estimand: the case-weighted mean difference allowing dependence within the six designed (data set, radius) groups with nested $N$. Interval: cluster-robust $t$ interval with the bias-reduced CR2 variance and a $t$ distribution with $G-1=5$ degrees of freedom ($G=6$ clusters), and a restricted wild-cluster bootstrap-$t$ TOST (CR2-studentized, all $6^6$ draws of Webb's six-point weights enumerated); equivalence at this level requires both.
\end{enumerate}
The \emph{minimal margin} of a level, $m_{\min}=\max(\lvert\ell\rvert,\lvert u\rvert)$ for the 90\% interval $[\ell,u]$, is the smallest margin at which equivalence holds: it holds for every $m>m_{\min}$ and for none smaller.
\end{definition}
Non-superiority (``$A$ gives no gain larger than $m$ over $B$'') needs only one of the two tests: the lower end of the 90\% interval must lie above $-m$. A result that is neither equivalent nor significantly different is inconclusive. The margin $m=0.05$~pp is a post hoc convention; the minimal margins let readers apply their own. The calibration of the bootstrap TOST, the joint resampling of the seeds (seed $k$ seeds the same random stream in every case) and an equal weighting of the clusters are reported in Section~\ref{S-sec:S-th-equiv} and Tables~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-tostaudit}, \ref{S-tab:S-sa-syncseed} and~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-eqclus}.
% CHECK-FINAL [C49]-[C52]: margin_pp == EQ_MARGIN; TOST: 90 % bootstrap CI inside (-m, m); Bayes signed-rank, rope (-m, m); min_margin_pp in tab:equivalence

Model-shift margin justification (X29/X30) deliberately dropped (D13).

\emph{Sensitivity of the inference.} Six qualification thresholds (1--30 feasible runs), dropping the cases with an imputed maximal difference and keeping zero differences change no verdict; cluster analyses over the six (data set, radius) clusters qualify only the significant contrasts of the salp-swarm hybrids with the controls; and one Holm family over all 28 case-mean tests makes only PSO-VNS vs.\ PSO on the cases with $N\ge 10$ and PSO-VNS ($\omega=0.5$) vs.\ PSO-VNS ($\omega=0.9$) nonsignificant (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-inference}; Tables~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-imputation} and~\ref{S-tab:X-threshold}--\ref{S-tab:X-multiplicity}).

\subsection{Verification and Reproducibility}
\label{sec:validation}
Every table, figure and number of the article and the supplement is generated from the stored per-run records by the analysis scripts, and every outcome-dependent statement is linked to an automated check. Table~\ref{tab:verification} lists what was verified. The diagnostics rerun stored runs with instrumented copies of the optimizers, which differ from the code of the paper only by logging lines that read the state and draw no random numbers. Not re-executed were the optimization runs themselves, the instrumented diagnostic reruns (their stored outputs pass the diagnostic checks) and the calibration runs of the reference implementation (recomputed from their stored outputs) (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-archive}).

The reproducibility package (Data availability; Section~\ref{S-sec:S-archive}) contains all records, models, optimizers and analysis scripts, a build script, a README with the command behind every run file and every table, figure and number, checksums and a Dockerfile; it is available from the corresponding author upon reasonable request.

\subsection{Computational Cost}
\label{sec:literature}
\label{sec:runtime}
Table~\ref{tab:runtime} gives the elapsed times. Per evaluation, the metaheuristics need similar times and MS-SLSQP more than every metaheuristic, so at equal budgets an MS-SLSQP run takes longer; the feasibility-preserving initialization, which is not charged, takes longer than a 6,030-evaluation PSO-VNS run. Times from separate batches are indicative. Published Kusiak--Song results~\cite{Kusiak2010,Eroglu2012,Eroglu2013,Bansal2017} use other budgets, constraint handling and reporting; they are discussed, not compared numerically, in Section~\ref{S-sec:S-literature}.

\section{Wind Farm Type I: Kusiak--Song Benchmark}
\label{sec:results}
\label{sec:overall}
\label{sec:farm-I}
Type~I is the Kusiak--Song benchmark~\cite{Kusiak2010}: 68 circular farms under two synthetic wind roses, on which the nine methods are compared at one budget. This section gives the experimental setting, the numerical results and their statistical analysis (including the primary comparison of PSO-VNS with PSO and an all-run endpoint), followed by studies of the budget and initialization (Section~\ref{sec:beyond}) and of the model assumptions (Section~\ref{sec:robustness}).

\subsection{Experimental Setting}
The setting is that of Type~I in Table~\ref{tab:farm-types}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Cases}: 68, the two wind data sets combined with three farm radii, $r=500$~m with $N=2,\ldots,10$ turbines (9 cases per data set), $r=750$~m with $N=2,\ldots,12$ (11) and $r=1000$~m with $N=2,\ldots,15$ (14).
\item \emph{Wind data} (Section~\ref{sec:wind}; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-wind}): Data Set~I, a nearly unidirectional rose with direction-independent wind speeds (Weibull scale 13~m/s in all directions), and Data Set~II, a broad rose with direction-dependent wind speeds (scales 2.6--10~m/s); both with 24 direction bins of 15$^\circ$, Weibull shape 2 and 21 speed bins of 0.5~m/s between cut-in and rated speed.
\item \emph{Model} (Section~\ref{sec:model}): the Jensen top-hat wake ($K=0.075$, $C_T=0.8$, hub-center test, root-sum-square superposition) and the linear Kusiak--Song power curve (cut-in 3.5~m/s, rated 14~m/s, 1500~kW; $D=77$~m); the objective is the wake loss in percent of the wake-free value.
\item \emph{Constraints} (Section~\ref{sec:constraints}): minimum spacing $4D=308$~m and the circular boundary of radius $r$, handled by the penalty~\eqref{eq:penalty} with box clipping to $[-r,r]^{2N}$.
\item \emph{Runs}: 6{,}030 objective evaluations per run, random initialization and 30 seed-paired runs (seeds 1--30) per case and method, 18{,}360 runs in total.
\item \emph{Methods}: the nine methods of Section~\ref{sec:setup-methods} with the settings of Table~\ref{tab:params}.
\item \emph{Analysis}: the feasibility-aware ranking and the tests of Section~\ref{sec:setup-stats}; per-case results in Tables~\ref{S-tab:res-1-500}--\ref{S-tab:res-2-1000} (GA: Table~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-ga}).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Numerical Results}

Wake loss: per case the mean wake loss of the feasible runs, averaged over the cases of the cluster in which the method has at least 15 of 30 feasible runs (superscript: number of such cases when fewer than all; such means are over easier case subsets and are not comparable). Table~\ref{tab:farm-I-res} reports the mean wake loss of the nine methods for each farm size (500, 750 and 1000~m) under both wind data sets and over all 68 cases, with the share of feasible runs and the average rank. PSO-VNS has the best average rank, followed by PSO and GA, and the lowest mean wake loss in the three Data Set~II clusters and over all cases; PSO has the lowest in Data Set~I at 500~m and GA at 750 and 1000~m. PSO-VNS ends feasible in all runs (DE: 71.4\%); in the densest cases (500~m, $N=10$), the global best of its PSO phase is feasible at the switch in only 63.3\% of the runs, and the VNS phase makes all runs feasible. The wake loss grows with $N$, falls as the farm becomes larger, and is higher for the broader wind rose of Data Set~II.

W/T/L: cases in which PSO-VNS is significantly better / not different / worse in the seed-paired Wilcoxon test of the 30 runs (infeasible runs below every feasible run; Holm over the 8 comparisons of PSO-VNS in each case, $\alpha=0.05$).}
\end{table}
The Friedman test rejects equal performance of the nine methods (Table~\ref{tab:farm-I-stats}). PSO-VNS is significantly better than the other six methods in both the average-rank test and the case-mean Wilcoxon test, but not significantly different from PSO or GA. GA and PSO are practically equivalent at $\pm0.05$~pp at the case level (exploratory), whereas equivalence of GA and PSO-VNS is not shown (90\% intervals of GA minus PSO and GA minus PSO-VNS: [$-$0.022, 0.041] and [$-$0.006, 0.062]~pp; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-ga}).

\subsubsection{PSO-VNS versus PSO}
\label{sec:psovns-pso}
Whether the VNS phase adds a practically relevant gain to PSO is a primary question (Section~\ref{sec:setup-stats}). It is decided by equivalence at the margin $m=\pm0.05$~pp (Definition~\ref{def:S-equiv}; Table~\ref{tab:equiv-main} and Fig.~\ref{fig:equiv-curve}); Table~\ref{tab:psovns-pso} gives the further tests and the subsets of cases. At the run level PSO-VNS has more significant wins than losses (13/52/3; Table~\ref{tab:farm-I-stats}), but the case means do not differ significantly. The findings are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Equivalent on average.} The mean difference, $-0.018$~pp, has a 90\% interval inside $\pm0.05$~pp at the seed level, at which the verdict is decided, and at the case level, but not at the cluster level (six scenario clusters). The seed-level interval excludes zero: the two methods differ reproducibly on this benchmark, but by less than the margin (0.020\% of AEP). Practical equivalence is also the most probable outcome of the Bayesian signed-rank test.
\item \emph{Differences in both directions.} 19 cases differ by more than the margin, 11 in favor of PSO-VNS and 8 in favor of PSO; the two methods are equivalent for $N<10$, but not for $N\ge10$. Post hoc, PSO-VNS has the lower mean wake loss in all 10 Data Set~II cases with $N\ge10$, whereas the 7 Data Set~I cases beyond the margin, dense layouts in the two smaller farms, all favor PSO; this crossover has no identified cause and is a hypothesis for designed experiments.
\item \emph{Second phase.} After the switch both methods keep improving (Fig.~\ref{S-fig:S-conv-max}): the VNS phase removes slightly more of the remaining wake loss than continuing PSO for the same 3,000 evaluations, and it repairs the 26 runs infeasible at the switch.
\end{enumerate}
PSO-VNS thus does not dominate the standard PSO: it is at least as good on average, gains in larger Data Set~II layouts and loses in dense Data Set~I layouts. With 1$^\circ$ direction bins, its advantage is small but significant and equivalence is not shown (Table~\ref{tab:equiv-main}); optimized directly with 1$^\circ$ bins, its advantage exceeds the margin (Section~\ref{sec:direction}).

\subsubsection{All-Run Reliability and Coordinate Precision}
\label{sec:archive-reliability}
The feasibility-aware rank compares conditional means and can favor a method with a better mean among fewer feasible runs: on Lillgrund at 6,030 evaluations, PSO-VNS has 17/30 feasible runs and a conditional mean AEP of 113.074~GWh, MS-SLSQP 30/30 and 112.865~GWh. We therefore add an all-run paired endpoint, in which for each seed pair
\begin{enumerate}
\item a feasible run beats an infeasible one;
\item of two feasible runs, the better objective wins (ties within $10^{-9}$);
\item two infeasible runs tie.
\end{enumerate}
Over $n_s$ seed pairs with $n_W$ wins and $n_T$ ties, the score is $(n_W+n_T/2)/n_s$ (0.5: no difference). Table~\ref{tab:allrun} gives it for the nine methods. On the benchmark, PSO-VNS is better more often than not against every method, also with the 30 synchronized seeds as the unit of replication; this frequency does not measure the size of the differences, and against PSO the mean difference lies within the margin (Table~\ref{tab:equiv-main}). SSA-VNS loses slightly more often than it wins against RSD-VNS. On Lillgrund, PSO-VNS is better than PSO at both budgets and than GA at 6,030 evaluations (at 30,030 the GA comparison is not significant), but not better than MS-SLSQP at 6,030 evaluations.

\emph{Coordinate precision.} The stored coordinates are rounded to three (benchmark, IEA37) or two decimals (Horns Rev~1, Lillgrund). Of the 56,540 stored records, the rounded coordinates confirm 50,826 feasibility labels at $10^{-6}$~m and contradict none (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-precision}); rerun with a 17-digit writer, 4,794 of the other 5,714 are confirmed (0 changed). The remaining 920 are SLSQP-based (SciPy's SLSQP is not bit-reproducible across CPU and BLAS builds), among them 220 of the 240 exact-gradient runs; they keep the labels reported by the runs, which the audit neither confirms nor contradicts. Taking every open label as infeasible leaves the order of the nine benchmark methods unchanged (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-sa-feasbounds}). Replayed from the rounded coordinates, 905 of the 51,810 feasible records differ from their stored objective by more than 0.05~pp where the hub-center wake test makes the objective jump, among them 233 of the 23,728 benchmark layouts re-evaluated in Section~\ref{sec:robustness}; ranked by the replayed objective, the nine methods and the site means keep their order (PSO-VNS${}-{}$PSO: $-0.018$~pp, unchanged). All writers of the reproducibility package store 17 significant digits.

\label{sec:beyond}
\subsubsection{Budget Scaling}
\label{sec:budget}

The budget study covers the six largest benchmark cases with the nine methods and the ablation variants LX-SSA-VNS and RS-VNS (Table~\ref{tab:feasbudget}; Fig.~\ref{fig:budget}). From random starts:
\begin{itemize}
\item PSO-VNS has the best average rank at 6,030 and 30,030 evaluations (2.00 and 1.83; GA 2.67 and 2.00), and GA at 120,030 evaluations (2.17; PSO-VNS 3.00). PSO-VNS ranks first in 2, 2 and 1 of the six cases, GA in 2, 4 and 4; in the per-case run-level tests (Holm), GA is better than PSO-VNS in 3 of the six cases at 30,030 and in 4 at 120,030 evaluations, and PSO-VNS is better than GA in none (Table~\ref{S-tab:budget-cases}).
\item PSO's mean wake loss minus that of PSO-VNS is $+0.10$, $+0.18$ and $+0.27$~pp at the three budgets: the advantage of the VNS phase over continued PSO grows with the budget.
\item The methods differ mainly in how much they gain from a larger budget: from 6,030 to 120,030 evaluations, the mean wake loss of GA falls from 4.28\% to 2.97\%, that of VNS from 4.95\% to 3.17\%, of PSO-VNS from 4.08\% to 3.14\% and of PSO only from 4.18\% to 3.42\%; PSO falls back from an average rank of 2.33 to 6.83.
\item At 120,030 evaluations, GA has the lowest mean wake loss (0.17~pp below PSO-VNS), and VNS, SSA-VNS, RS-VNS, LX-SSA-VNS and DE come within 0.17~pp of PSO-VNS.
\end{itemize}
PSO-VNS is thus the best method at the benchmark budget and at five times this budget; with twenty times the budget, GA, which keeps improving, is better.

\subsubsection{Feasibility-Preserving Initialization}
\label{sec:feasinit}
With feasibility-preserving initialization (Table~\ref{tab:feasbudget}; per case: Table~\ref{S-tab:feasinit-cases}), the share of feasible runs rises to 100.0\% for SSA-VNS (from 95.0\%), GA (85.6\%), SSA (80.0\%), LX-SSA (77.8\%) and DE (1.1\%).
\begin{itemize}
\item VNS alone then has the best average rank (1.50) and GA the lowest mean wake loss (3.76\%; VNS 3.81\%); PSO-VNS ranks third (3.50; loss 3.94\%, 4.08\% with random starts).
\item PSO and DE never improve on the best initial layout (210 runs each; Table~\ref{S-tab:D-feasstart}): their moves change all or most coordinates at once, and in our runs no such trial was both feasible and better (Lemma~\ref{S-lem:S-feas-improve} and Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-frozen}; diagnostics: Section~\ref{S-sec:S-diag-feas}). VNS, which moves one coordinate at a time, and GA, which leaves its best initial layout in all 210 runs, are not affected.
\end{itemize}
The advantage of PSO-VNS thus depends on the initialization: from random starts, its PSO phase helps mainly by reaching feasible layouts.

\subsection{Sensitivity to the Model Assumptions}
\label{sec:robustness}
% tab:robust-final is placed in the main text; the full re-evaluation stays in the supplement (Table S-robust). tab:F-bench and tab:F-equiv are
% captured in 06_type1.tex (\CaptureSuppTables{analysis/mpce_supp_direction.tex}).
The benchmark (Type~I) fixes five modeling choices. We tested each of them with the nine methods:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{power curve} and
\item \emph{wake model}: every feasible final layout re-evaluated, not re-optimized (Table~\ref{tab:robust-final}; all methods: Table~\ref{S-tab:robust}), which asks whether the delivered layouts keep their order under another model;
\item \emph{minimum spacing}: methods re-optimized at $5D$ and $6D$ on the split cases;
\item \emph{boundary rule}: five methods re-optimized under two other constraint handlings on six cases;
\item \emph{direction resolution}: final layouts re-evaluated with finer wind roses, and the fine rose optimized directly on eight cases (Table~\ref{tab:direction}).
\end{enumerate}
Re-evaluations use the stored rounded coordinates (Section~\ref{sec:archive-reliability}).

\subsubsection{Power Curve}
\label{sec:powercurve}
A cubic power curve between cut-in and rated speed~\cite{Carrillo2013} (also with a 25~m/s cut-out) lowers the expected power by 17.7\% (Data Set~I) and 36.9\% (Data Set~II), so our energy values are benchmark values, but it leaves the ranking intact ($\bar\tau=0.94$; same best method in 90\% of the cases; PSO-VNS first, PSO second and GA third, also with the cut-out). The $\pm0.05$~pp equivalence verdicts (Sections~\ref{sec:psovns-pso} and~\ref{sec:ablation}) were evaluated under the linear benchmark curve only.
% CHECK-FINAL [C21]: robustness.Cubic/CubicCutout: tau >= 0.9, PSO-VNS rank position 1

\subsubsection{Wake Model}
\label{sec:gaussian}
The uncalibrated Gaussian wake of~\cite{Bastankhah2014} ($K_{\rm G}=0.04$; Section~\ref{S-sec:S-gauss}) differs from the Jensen cone ($K=0.075$) in its smooth lateral profile and expansion rate and gives no deficit to turbines upstream of the wake source. It changes the wake loss of the same final layouts by 0.38~pp on average (median 0.28~pp), 7.6 times the equivalence margin (Section~\ref{sec:setup-stats}), and by more than the margin for 86\% of the 17{,}626 layouts, whereas the per-case difference PSO-VNS${}-{}$PSO changes by only 0.031~pp on average. The ordering is less stable ($\bar\tau=0.66$): PSO is narrowly first (average rank 2.24; PSO-VNS 2.31, GA 3.09), but not with 1$^\circ$ bins (Table~\ref{tab:direction}).
% CHECK-EXTRA [X30]: Jensen vs Gaussian wake loss of the same layouts: mean \NXModelShiftMean pp (median \NXModelShiftMedian; \NXModelShiftRatio x margin; > margin for \NXModelShiftAboveMarginPct % of \NXModelShiftNLayouts layouts); per-case PSO-VNS - PSO changes by \NXModelShiftPairMean pp on average (below the margin)
% CHECK-FINAL [C22]: robustness.Gauss (15 deg): tau < 0.8, PSOC first, PSOBV second (nine-method pool with GA: 2.24 vs 2.31, rev5_ga_numbers_robust.tex); CHECK-DIR [F09]: PSO-VNS first under the Gaussian wake at 1 deg

\subsubsection{Minimum Spacing}
\label{sec:spacing}
The optimized layouts often lie on the $4D$ constraint: 49\% of the feasible final layouts also satisfy $5D$ and 36\% $6D$ (4.1\% and 0.1\% for $N=11$--15, where the fixed site leaves less room), so they do not transfer to larger spacings without re-optimization (site capacity: Table~\ref{S-tab:capacity}).
% CHECK-FINAL [C23]: spacing_share.n11_15.pct_5d < 10 ("often lie on the 4D constraint")
% verified (manual): shares 49 % / 36 % (all N) vs 4.1 % / 0.1 % (N = 11-15) from mpce_numbers.tex (\NSp...); constructive capacity at 4D/5D/6D: Table S-capacity

The nine methods and RSD-VNS were re-optimized at $5D$ and $6D$ on the 12 split cases, of which the two with $r=500$~m, $N=10$ admit no layout at these spacings, so the same 10 cases are compared at every spacing (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-xp-spacing}; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-spacing}):
\begin{itemize}
\item PSO-VNS has the best average rank at $4D$ and $6D$ (1.70 and 2.10) and the second best at $5D$ (2.20), where GA ranks first (1.90); at $6D$, GA is feasible in only 62.7\% of its runs and ranks sixth (5.10). The rank differences between PSO-VNS and GA are not significant (Holm). The mean advantage of PSO-VNS over PSO is larger at $5D$ and $6D$ ($-0.094$ and $-0.068$~pp, the latter over the 8 cases in which PSO has at least 15 feasible runs) than at $4D$ ($-0.035$~pp), with 90\% intervals below zero at the seed and the case level at $5D$ and $6D$.
\item MS-SLSQP moves up with the spacing (average rank 6.50, 5.10 and 3.40; second at $6D$).
\item The SSA phase gives no gain over disc sampling at $5D$ (SSA-VNS${}-{}$RSD-VNS: $+0.043$~pp, case-level 90\% CI [$-$0.011, 0.103]), but at $6D$ SSA-VNS has the lower mean wake loss in all 8 cases in which both qualify ($-0.136$~pp, [$-$0.215, $-$0.073]; Wilcoxon $p=0.0078$): the no-gain finding of Section~\ref{sec:ablation} holds at $4D$ and $5D$, not at $6D$.
\item On the all-run endpoint (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-allrun-spacing}), PSO-VNS scores 0.593 and 0.613 against PSO at $5D$ and $6D$ (95\% case-bootstrap intervals [0.497, 0.693] and [0.548, 0.670]) and 0.567 and 0.653 against GA ([0.507, 0.627] and [0.553, 0.753]), but 0.580 [0.432, 0.733] against MS-SLSQP at $6D$: no advantage.
\end{itemize}
With 8 to 10 cases, these are estimates for the split cases, not general effects.

\subsubsection{Boundary Rule and Constraint Handling}
\label{sec:boundary}
All methods clip the coordinates to the bounding square and leave the circle to the penalty (Section~\ref{sec:constraints}). The rule matters: an SSA that projects turbines radially onto the circle instead attains higher mean objectives than the original SSA in all six representative cases ($p\le0.0036$).
% CHECK-FINAL [C41]: boundary_rule n_higher == n_cases == 6 and max_p < 0.01
PSO-VNS, PSO, GA, SSA-VNS and DE were therefore rerun on six cases (seeds 31--60, 6,030 evaluations; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-constraint}) under Deb's feasibility rules on the dimensionless violations $g^{\rm b}_i/r^2$ and $g^{\rm s}_{ij}/\ell_{\min}$ (reference runs under the penalty reproduce the stored runs bit for bit):
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{With radial projection}, the conditional wake loss of every method fell by 0.46--0.80~pp and the feasibility of DE rose from 33.3\% to 80.0\%; the run-level order PSO-VNS, PSO, GA, SSA-VNS, DE and the leading case-level method (PSO-VNS) persisted; PSO-VNS kept its all-run advantage over GA, SSA-VNS and DE (0.683, 0.850, 0.844), but not over PSO (0.561; conditional $\overline{\Delta L}=-0.095$~pp [$-0.162$, $-0.037$]).
\item \emph{With box clipping}, the feasibility of PSO fell from 98.9\% to 73.3\%, the conditional loss of PSO-VNS rose by 0.23~pp, its all-run advantage over GA (0.517) and SSA-VNS (0.583) was not significant, and GA had the best case-level rank (1.83 vs.\ 2.17; run level: PSO-VNS 2.19, GA 2.28).
\end{itemize}
With the 30 seeds as the unit of replication, every all-run verdict is unchanged (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-dep-studies}). By the survival rule stated beforehand, the conclusions fail with box clipping and, for the all-run advantage of PSO-VNS over PSO, with projection: the comparison is one of search strategies under a common penalty and repair, which is not neutral across methods.

\subsubsection{Direction Resolution}
\label{sec:direction}
Because a pair of turbines can be placed with no bin center in its narrow wake interval (Section~\ref{sec:wind}), we split each bin into 3 (5$^\circ$) or 15 (1$^\circ$) sub-bins with its Weibull parameters and an equal share of its frequency (wake-free power unchanged) and re-evaluated all 23{,}728 feasible final layouts of the benchmark methods (Tables~\ref{tab:direction} and~\ref{S-tab:F-bench}).
\begin{table}[!htb]
\centering
\caption{Direction resolution on Type~I: average rank of the three leading methods when the final layouts are re-evaluated with finer wind roses (nine methods, 68 cases) and when the 1$^\circ$ objective is optimized directly (five methods, eight cases).}
\label{tab:direction}
\footnotesize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\begin{tabular}{llcccl}
\toprule
Wind rose & Wake model & PSO-VNS & PSO & GA & Best \\
\midrule
15$^\circ$ (benchmark) & Jensen & 2.18 & 2.40 & 2.74 & PSO-VNS \\
1$^\circ$, re-evaluated & Jensen & 2.26 & 2.57 & 4.01 & PSO-VNS \\
15$^\circ$ (benchmark) & Gaussian & 2.31 & 2.24 & 3.09 & PSO \\
1$^\circ$, re-evaluated & Gaussian & 2.49 & 2.56 & 3.96 & PSO-VNS \\
1$^\circ$, optimized (8 cases) & Jensen & 1.12 & 2.62 & 3.38 & PSO-VNS \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\par\vspace{2pt}\parbox{\columnwidth}{\scriptsize Re-evaluated rows: feasibility-aware average rank among the nine methods (all ranks: Table~\ref{S-tab:F-bench}). Optimized row: PSO-VNS, PSO, GA, MS-SLSQP and RSD-VNS optimized with 1$^\circ$ bins on eight cases, seeds 31--60, 6,030 evaluations (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-fine}).}
\end{table}
\begin{itemize}
\item The 1$^\circ$ rose raises the mean wake loss of every method, by 0.40~pp (DE) to 1.09~pp (GA; PSO-VNS: 1.322\% to 2.335\%), mostly already with 5$^\circ$ sub-bins: optimized layouts exploit the gaps between the coarse bins, those of GA, PSO and the VNS-based methods most.
\item PSO-VNS still ranks first, also under the Gaussian wake at 1$^\circ$; PSO is the only method not significantly different. Beyond the top two the order changes ($\bar\tau=0.50$; same best method in 31\% of the cases): GA falls to third with the largest loss rise, and MS-SLSQP moves from seventh to fourth; its gap to PSO-VNS shrinks from 0.435 to 0.135~pp but remains significant.
\item PSO-VNS has a lower case-mean wake loss than GA at 5$^\circ$ and 1$^\circ$ ($-0.098$ and $-0.105$~pp, Holm $p\le3.1\times10^{-7}$), whereas at 15$^\circ$ the difference ($-0.026$~pp) is not significant.
\item For PSO-VNS vs.\ PSO (details: Table~\ref{S-tab:F-equiv}), the case-mean difference becomes $-0.043$~pp (90\% CI [$-0.062$, $-0.025$]; $p=2.5\times10^{-4}$; the same with 5$^\circ$ sub-bins): a small but significant advantage, not equivalence at $\pm0.05$~pp (minimal margin 0.062~pp). On the 44 non-trivial cases, equivalence is shown at neither resolution, and the Data Set~II $N\ge10$ advantage remains (10 of 10 cases).
\item The ablation conclusions hold, but the small SSA gain over RS-VNS is not significant with 1$^\circ$ bins (Table~\ref{S-tab:F-abl}).
\end{itemize}
The order beyond the top two and the equivalence verdict are thus properties of the discretized benchmark; the first place of PSO-VNS is not.
% CHECK-DIR [F03]: loss rise of every method, PSO-VNS 1.322 -> 2.335; [F04]: most of the change already with 5-deg sub-bins (> 70 % for PSO-VNS); [F05]: VNS-based methods and PSO rise most, DE/MS-SLSQP least ("VNS-based methods most"); [F06]: PSO-VNS first at 5 and 1 deg, PSO the only one not significantly different (Holm); [F09]: Gaussian: PSO first at 15 deg, PSO-VNS first at 1 deg; [F07]: MS-SLSQP 7th -> 4th with GA in the pool (6th -> 3rd without GA), tau 0.52, same best 37 %; [F08]: MS-SLSQP gap shrinks from 0.435 to 0.135 pp, still significant (Holm)
% CHECK-DIR [F10]: PSO-VNS - PSO -0.043, CI [-0.062, -0.025], not equivalent, p < 0.05; [F11]: same with 5-deg sub-bins; [F12]: per-case sign changes in 26 of 60 cases; [F13]: non-trivial cases: equivalence at neither resolution; [F14]: DS II N >= 10 10/10; [F16]: component-analysis conclusions hold (SSA-VNS and LX-SSA-VNS worse than RSD-VNS, PSO-VNS better than RSD-VNS, RS-VNS and VNS, PSO-VNS best of nine); [F17]: SSA-VNS vs RS-VNS not significant at 1 deg
% Page budget: kept in the supplementary material: \SuppTable{tab:F-bench}

\emph{Direct optimization with 1$^\circ$ bins.} PSO-VNS, PSO, GA, MS-SLSQP and RSD-VNS also optimized the 1$^\circ$ objective directly on eight cases (both data sets, $N=6$--15; seeds 31--60; 6,030 evaluations), compared with a matched control arm optimized with 15$^\circ$ bins and evaluated with 1$^\circ$ bins (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-fine}).
\begin{itemize}
\item Optimizing with 1$^\circ$ bins lowers the 1$^\circ$ wake loss on the same seeds by 0.84--1.12~pp for the metaheuristics and by 0.32~pp for MS-SLSQP, which drops from second to last.
\item PSO-VNS ranks first in both arms (direct: 1.12; PSO 2.62, GA 3.38, RSD-VNS 3.62, MS-SLSQP 4.25; control: 2.25).
\item In the direct arm it has a lower wake loss than PSO in all eight cases ($-0.194$~pp; 90\% CI [$-0.234$, $-0.152$] at the seed and [$-0.272$, $-0.114$] at the case level), beyond the margin and about twice its advantage in the control arm ($-0.086$~pp; difference-in-differences on the 238 of 240 case--seed pairs where all four runs are feasible: $-0.115$~pp, exact sign-flip $p=3.9\times10^{-4}$; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-dep-studies}).
\item Its all-run scores against the other four methods in the direct arm are 0.70--0.87, all significant with the 30 seeds as the unit of replication ($p_{\rm Holm}\le3.2\times10^{-6}$).
\end{itemize}
The leader thus does not change when the search uses 1$^\circ$ bins.

\section{Wind Farm Type II: Horns Rev~1 Block}
\label{sec:hornsrev-site}
\label{sec:farm-II}
This section compares the nine methods on a 16-turbine block of the Horns Rev~1 offshore wind farm, the first of the two farm types with a measured wind climate.

\subsection{Experimental Setting}
The settings specific to Type~II are (Table~\ref{tab:farm-types}; details: Section~\ref{S-sec:S-hrmodel}):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Site and layout.} The 16-turbine corner block of PyWake's Horns Rev~1 example~\cite{PyWake}; its installed layout, a regular $7D$ grid, is the reference.
\item \emph{Turbines.} V80 ($D=80$~m) with the power and thrust curves of the example.
\item \emph{Wind data.} The measured 12-sector Weibull climate of the example, in 5$^\circ$ direction bins (six per sector) and 1-m/s speed bins (3--25~m/s); wake-free AEP 148.81~GWh/yr.
\item \emph{Wake model.} Jensen with $K=0.04$, a hub-center in-wake test, root-sum-square superposition and the thrust coefficient $C_T$ at the free-stream speed.
\item \emph{Constraints.} Boundary: the parallelogram through the corner turbines (enlarged by 0.1\%); the boundary violation in~\eqref{eq:penalty} is the distance (m) outside it. Minimum spacing $\ell_{\min}=4D$, as on Type~I (installed: $7D$).
\item \emph{Budgets, seeds and initialization.} 6{,}030 and 30{,}030 evaluations; 30 seed-paired runs (seeds 1--30) per method and budget; random initialization.
\item \emph{Methods.} The nine methods with the settings of Table~\ref{tab:params}, except that the search box is the square of half-width 943~m centered on the block, and this half-width is the $r$ of the VNS radii. The ablation variants LX-SSA-VNS and RS-VNS were also run (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-hr-eval}).
\end{enumerate}
For the installed 80-turbine farm at identical bins, PyWake~2.6.20's NOJ model~\cite{PyWake} gives 661.39~GWh/yr and our model 666.75~GWh/yr (0.81\% more; wake loss 10.39\% (ours) vs.\ 11.11\% (PyWake); Table~\ref{S-tab:F-pywake}); with $C_T$ at the local speed, our model reproduces PyWake's hub-center NOJ (momentum-theory induction) exactly, and rotor averaging does not explain the difference.
% CHECK-DIR [F31] [F32]: PyWake 2.6.20 NOJ(k=0.04) at bins identical to ours (same wake-free AEP): 661.39 vs ours 666.75 GWh/yr (+0.81 %), wake loss 10.39 % (ours) vs 11.11 % (PyWake)
% CHECK-DIR [F33] [F34]: rotor averaging does not explain the difference; with C_T at the local (waked) speed our model reproduces PyWake's NOJ with hub-centre test (momentum-theory induction) to 1e-6 GWh/yr; the old 662.5 constant (\NHRPyWakeDiff) is dropped [F36]
% CHECK-DIR [F35]: installed 16-turbine block: PyWake NOJ 139.08 vs ours 139.82 GWh/yr (+0.53 %)

At 6,030 evaluations, the main difference is feasibility: PSO-VNS is feasible in all 30 runs, PSO in only 19 and DE in none (Table~\ref{tab:farm-II}). PSO-VNS ranks first at 6,030 evaluations and GA at 30,030, where PSO-VNS ranks second; at 6,030 evaluations, no random-start run of any method, GA included, reaches the installed block (best layouts: Figs.~\ref{S-fig:S-hr16} and~\ref{S-fig:S-layouts-sites}).
% CHECK-FINAL [C20]: PSO feasible in fewer than 30 of 30 random-start runs ("only"); [C31]: 6,030 PSO-VNS mean AEP below the installed block; no 6,030 random-start run above the installed block with 5-deg bins (Table S-F-hr; 16 runs above at 5 deg over all budgets, 0 at 1 deg: [F21])
Re-evaluated with 1$^\circ$ bins (Table~\ref{S-tab:F-hr}), the installed block loses 0.32~GWh/yr and the optimized layouts 1.08~GWh/yr on average, the VNS-based methods most: like those of the benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:direction}), the optimized layouts exploit the gaps between the coarse direction bins, and none of the 961 feasible optimized layouts (all methods and budgets) exceeds the installed block with 1$^\circ$ bins (22 with 5$^\circ$ bins).

The former "runs exceed the installed layout" (C30) and the 6,030 run-level test / highest mean AEP (C19, C39) are no longer asserted (R4-1, R4-10; D16, D19).

\subsection{Statistical Analysis}
In the seed-paired tests of Table~\ref{tab:farm-II}, PSO-VNS is better than seven of the eight methods at both budgets; it does not differ from GA at 6,030 evaluations, and GA is better at 30,030. Table~\ref{tab:farm-II-tests} adds the feasibility test against PSO and the tests against GA and PSO with the final layouts re-evaluated: all outcomes hold under the three evaluators except the AEP advantage of PSO-VNS over PSO at 6,030 evaluations, which is significant only with the 5$^\circ$ bins used in optimization.

\section{Wind Farm Type III: Lillgrund Block}
\label{sec:lillgrund}
\label{sec:farm-III}
This section repeats the comparison of Section~\ref{sec:hornsrev-site} on a second site with a measured wind climate, a block of the Lillgrund offshore wind farm with a tighter spacing.

\subsection{Experimental Setting}
The wake model, the direction and speed bins, the penalty, the budgets, seeds and initialization, and the method settings are those of Type~II (Section~\ref{sec:hornsrev-site}). The settings that differ are (Table~\ref{tab:farm-types}; details: Section~\ref{S-sec:S-xp-site} and Table~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-site}):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Site and layout.} The $4\times4$ block at the eastern corner of the Lillgrund offshore wind farm (Sweden); its installed layout is the reference.
\item \emph{Turbines.} Siemens SWT-2.3-93 ($D=93$~m, hub height 65~m) with the power and thrust curves of PyWake's Lillgrund example~\cite{PyWake}.
\item \emph{Wind data.} The 12-sector Weibull climate of that example, fitted to seven months of met-mast measurements at 61~m~\cite{Gocmen2016}; wake-free AEP 139.40~GWh/yr.
\item \emph{Constraints.} Boundary: the parallelogram through the corner turbines (enlarged by 0.2\%). Minimum spacing $\ell_{\min}=3D$, because 16 turbines do not fit into the block at $4D$ (installed minimum spacing: $3.29D$).
\item \emph{Methods.} The nine methods, GA included, at both budgets; the search box and the $r$ of the VNS radii follow the rule of Type~II (half-width: the largest absolute coordinate of the parallelogram). The control RSD-VNS, which here draws its Phase-1 samples in the parallelogram, was also run (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-site}).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Numerical Results}

Run-level Friedman test over the seeds: 6{,}030: $\chi^2_F=174.1$ (8 d.f.), $p=1.8\times10^{-33}$; 30{,}030: $\chi^2_F=96.3$ (8 d.f.), $p=2.4\times10^{-17}$.}
\end{table*}

PSO-VNS has the highest mean AEP at both budgets (Table~\ref{tab:farm-III}), but at 6,030 evaluations it is feasible in only 17 of 30 runs, and only MS-SLSQP is feasible in all 30. No method reaches the installed block on average; one PSO-VNS layout exceeds it by 0.05\%, also with 1$^\circ$ bins.

\subsection{Statistical Analysis}
In the seed-paired tests of Table~\ref{tab:farm-III}, which score feasibility first, MS-SLSQP is better than PSO-VNS at 6,030 evaluations, and at 30,030 evaluations PSO-VNS is better than every other method except VNS. The feasibility advantage of PSO-VNS over PSO is significant at both budgets, but with the final layouts re-evaluated with 1$^\circ$ bins, its lead over SSA-VNS, VNS and MS-SLSQP at 30,030 evaluations is not significant (against GA, the next-best mean, it is significant only through feasibility, 30 vs.\ 21 feasible runs), and MS-SLSQP has the higher mean AEP with 1$^\circ$ bins at 6,030 evaluations and under PyWake's NOJ model at both budgets (Table~\ref{tab:farm-III-tests}).

% begin optA/sw/06d_type4.tex
\section{Wind Farm Type IV: IEA37 Case Study~1}
\label{sec:iea37}
\label{sec:farm-IV}
This section compares the nine methods on the 16- and 36-turbine scenarios of IEA37 Case Study~1~\cite{Baker2019,IEA37repo}, which test optimizers rather than represent a site.

\subsection{Experimental Setting}
The settings specific to Type~IV are (Table~\ref{tab:farm-types}; details: Section~\ref{S-sec:S-iea37}):
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Sites.} Circular sites of radii 1300~m (16 turbines) and 2000~m (36 turbines); there is no installed layout, and the published layouts of the case study serve as references.
\item \emph{Turbines.} The reference turbine of the case study with its power curve.
\item \emph{Wind data.} The official wind rose: 16 directions at one wind speed.
\item \emph{Wake model.} The official simplified Bastankhah Gaussian wake; AEP in MWh as computed by the official calculator.
\item \emph{Constraints.} Circular boundary; minimum spacing $\ell_{\min}=2D$.
\item \emph{Budgets, seeds and initialization.} 6{,}030 and 30{,}030 evaluations; 30 seed-paired runs (seeds 1--30) per method and budget; random initialization.
\item \emph{Methods.} The nine methods with the settings of Table~\ref{tab:params}; as on Type~I, the search box is $[-r,r]^{2N}$ with the site radius $r$, which is also the $r$ of the VNS radii. The ablation variants LX-SSA-VNS and RS-VNS were also run (Table~\ref{S-tab:iea37-detail}); the two exact-gradient variants of SLSQP are described in Section~\ref{sec:iea-grad}.
\end{enumerate}
In the case study the participants chose their own optimizers and budgets~\cite{Baker2019}, so the published layouts are references, not results under the same budget.

\subsection{Numerical Results}

PSO-VNS ranks first with 16 turbines and GA with 36 turbines, at both budgets (Table~\ref{tab:farm-IV}). With 36 turbines, MS-SLSQP is feasible in only 4 and 14 of 30 runs: every forward-difference gradient costs $2N+1$ evaluations, so 6,030 evaluations allow at most 82 gradients. None of the methods reaches the best feasible published layouts (all gaps: Table~\ref{S-tab:F-iea-gap}); as the participants' budgets are not reported comparably, the size of the gap is indicative only.
% CHECK-FINAL [C40]: best of all methods below the best feasible published layout (strict) in both scenarios

\subsection{Statistical Analysis}
In the seed-paired tests of Table~\ref{tab:farm-IV}, PSO-VNS is better than all eight other methods with 16 turbines at 6,030 evaluations and than all but GA at 30,030. With 36 turbines GA is better than PSO-VNS at both budgets, and PSO-VNS does not differ significantly from VNS at either budget.

\subsection{Exact Gradients on IEA37}
\label{sec:iea-grad}
As the IEA37 model has an analytic AEP gradient, we also ran SLSQP with exact objective and constraint derivatives, as multistart (exact-gradient MS-SLSQP) and after the PSO phase of PSO-VNS (PSO-SLSQP), each gradient charged $c_g=3$ evaluations, its measured cost (30 seeds, random starts, both budgets; Section~\ref{S-sec:S-xp-grad}, Tables~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-grad} and~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-grad-calls}). In a joint pool of 13 methods (the nine methods, LX-SSA-VNS, RS-VNS and the two exact-gradient variants; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-iea-pool}), an exact-gradient variant ranks first in both scenarios and at both budgets and is better than each of the 11 methods without exact gradients in all 30 seed pairs ($p_{\rm Holm}=2.2\times10^{-8}$). Three qualifications apply:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The comparison tests complete implementations of the second phase, not the AEP derivative alone: the variants also differentiate the constraints (MS-SLSQP of the main comparison uses finite differences), PSO-SLSQP also restarts from the incumbent and personal bests, and the objective is only piecewise smooth.
\item The budget is equal in charged evaluations, not in time: per run the exact-gradient methods take 2.3--5.6 times as long as PSO-VNS (medians; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-time-sites}).
\item The result rests on the feasibility labels reported by the runs: the rounded stored coordinates cannot decide 220 of the 240 strict labels, and none is decided infeasible at any tolerance (Section~\ref{sec:archive-reliability}; Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-feasbounds}). If every open label were infeasible, PSO-VNS (16 turbines) or GA (36 turbines) would lead (Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-feasworst}).
\end{enumerate}

% end optA/sw/06d_type4.tex

\section{Ablation Study of PSO-VNS}
\label{sec:ablation}

% figures are not placed); tab:switch and tab:equivalence come from the global capture of

\begingroup\RenewEnviron{figure*}[1][]{}\endgroup% [flattened: removed \CaptureSuppTables{analysis/mpce_supp_diag.tex}]
% [flattened: removed \CaptureSuppTables{analysis/mpce_tab_split.tex}]
\makeatletter
% \AblTable[<placement>]{<label>}: like \SuppTable (placed once, TBD box if absent), with a placement of our choice
\newcommand{\AblTable}[2][!t]{\@ifundefined{supp@body@#2}{\SuppTable{#2}}{\@ifundefined{supp@used@#2}{%
  \global\@namedef{supp@used@#2}{}\begin{table}[#1]\@nameuse{supp@body@#2}\end{table}}{}}}
% posterior probabilities in math mode: "= value", or "< 0.01" / "> 0.99" for a bound (rule of \BayEq in the supplement)
\newcommand{\AblBayEq}[1]{\expandafter\abl@bayeq#1\@nil}
\def\abl@bayeq#1#2\@nil{\ifx#1\ensuremath #2\else =#1#2\fi}
\makeatother
The ablation isolates the components of PSO-VNS on the Type~I farms (68 cases, 6,030 evaluations, 30 seed-paired runs from random starts). It keeps the two-phase template (Section~\ref{sec:template}) and changes one component at a time:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Phase~1}: PSO is replaced by SSA (SSA-VNS), by LX-SSA (LX-SSA-VNS) or by random sampling of equal cost, uniform in the bounding square of the farm (RS-VNS) or in the farm disc (RSD-VNS), or removed (VNS alone);
\item \emph{Phase~2}: VNS is removed (PSO alone; SSA and LX-SSA alone serve as references for the VNS phase after a salp swarm);
\item \emph{budget split}: $\omega$ takes five values on twelve cases (Section~\ref{sec:split}).
\end{enumerate}
Table~\ref{tab:ablation} gives the average ranks of the nine variants, which differ significantly (Friedman test), and the planned contrasts; Fig.~\ref{fig:ablation} shows the convergence. A 90\% bootstrap interval of $\overline{\Delta L}$ inside $\pm0.05$~pp means practical equivalence at the case level (Table~\ref{tab:equiv-main}).

\subsection{The VNS Phase}
\label{sec:abl-vns}
The VNS phase clearly improves both salp-swarm methods (SSA-VNS vs.\ SSA and LX-SSA-VNS vs.\ LX-SSA in Table~\ref{tab:ablation}). After PSO, which PSO-VNS repeats for its first 3,030 evaluations (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-props}), its gain is small, not significant and practically equivalent to zero at the case level (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-pso}).
% CHECK-FINAL [C33]: VNS phase significant for SSA and LX-SSA (W > L, L = 0; case-mean p < 0.05, DL < 0), not for PSO (case-mean p >= 0.05, so also Holm); [C13]: PSOBV-PSOC W > L; [C49]: case-level 90 % CI \NEqPSOVNSvsPSOCI inside +-\NEqMargin

% Page budget: kept in the supplementary material: \AblTable{tab:D-vns}
At this budget the VNS phase is essentially one compass-search descent (Table~\ref{S-tab:D-vns}; 12 split cases $\times$ 10 seeds):
\begin{enumerate}
\item the first descent from the switch point gives nearly all of the Phase-2 improvement (PSO-VNS: 97.5\%);
\item the shake-and-search cycles take 21.5\% of the Phase-2 evaluations of PSO-VNS but give 0.5\% of its gain, and the few accepted cycles use only the first neighborhood ($k=1$);
\item for $N\ge12$ the median run never shakes and the first descent is unfinished in 39 of 40 runs, so the local-minimality guarantee (Proposition~\ref{S-prop:S-ls}) does not apply there;
\item VNS starts from the best feasible Phase-1 layout when one exists and never makes a feasible incumbent infeasible.
\end{enumerate}
The ablation thus measures the value of a local-search phase, a truncated compass search after Phase~1, in which shaking and neighborhood change can matter only for small $N$.
% CHECK-DIAG [D20]: cycles >= 15 % of the Phase-2 evaluations, < 5 % of the gain incl. the cut-off cycle, accepted cycles < 1 % (\NDCycleEvalsPctPSOVNS, median run \NDCycleEvalsPctMedianPSOVNS, \NDCycleSharePSOVNS, \NDCycleAllSharePSOVNS); [D08]: essentially one descent; [D09]: median run has no shaking step for N >= 12

\subsection{Random-Sampling Controls}
\label{sec:abl-controls}
RSD-VNS, RS-VNS and VNS alone are the controls. Sampling in the disc is the stronger control: RSD-VNS beats RS-VNS and ranks third of the nine variants (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}).
% CHECK-FINAL [C57]: RSDVNS-RSVNS L == 0, W > 0, case-mean p_Holm < 0.05, DL < 0; [C58]: RSD-VNS ranks third of \NAblNVar

% Page budget: kept in the supplementary material: \AblTable{tab:D-rsreplay}
RS-VNS is weak mainly because of the spacing constraint and only in part because of the square (Table~\ref{S-tab:D-rsreplay}, which replays its Phase~1): 730 of its runs draw no feasible sample, although all of them draw samples wholly inside the circle, and sampling in the disc would rescue only 242 of them.
% CHECK-FINAL [C60]: RS-VNS runs without a feasible sample (\NRSRunsNoFeasSample) = rescued by disc sampling (\NRsSquareExplains) + none either way (\NRsSpacingDominates), rescued < none; LEAD: extend C60 with runs_no_feasible_but_inside_sample == runs_no_feasible_sample (\NRsNoFeasInside = \NRSRunsNoFeasSample, mpce_results.py phase1_replay.RSVNS) for "all of them"
% CHECK-DIAG [D17]: replay agrees with the runs; inside rate matches (pi/4)^N; N = 15, 1000 m: \NDRsFeasNFifteen of \NDRsSamples box samples feasible; \NDRsRunsNoFeasSample runs (all runs of \NDRsCasesNoFeasSample cases) without a feasible sample; CHECK-FINAL [C60]: rescued \NRsSquareExplains < none either way \NRsSpacingDominates ("mainly because of the spacing")

% Page budget: kept in the supplementary material: \AblTable{tab:switch}
At the switch (Table~\ref{S-tab:switch}), the best layout is feasible in 75.7\% (RSD-VNS) and 64.2\% (RS-VNS) of the runs, well below the swarm phases, and disc sampling leaves 494 instead of 730 runs without a feasible sample; VNS must often repair the start. Beating RS-VNS is therefore only a minimum bar.

\subsection{Swarm First Phases}
\label{sec:abl-swarm}
A PSO phase clearly beats every control; the salp-swarm phases do not (Tables~\ref{tab:ablation} and~\ref{tab:equiv-main}):
\begin{enumerate}
\item PSO-VNS is significantly better than RSD-VNS, RS-VNS and VNS alone in most cases and significantly worse in at most one, and its gain over RSD-VNS lies wholly beyond the 0.05~pp margin;
\item SSA-VNS is significantly better than RSD-VNS in no case, and the case means, not the scenario clusters, slightly favor RSD-VNS (Hodges--Lehmann estimate $+0.025$~pp): a gain of the SSA phase larger than the margin is ruled out at the seed and the case level, whereas equivalence remains inconclusive;
\item LX-SSA-VNS is worse than RSD-VNS;
\item the small SSA gain over RS-VNS only reflects that control's weakness; LX-SSA-VNS and RS-VNS are practically equivalent.
\end{enumerate}
Re-evaluated with 1$^\circ$ direction bins (Table~\ref{S-tab:F-abl}), these findings hold, except that the SSA gain over RS-VNS is not significant with 1$^\circ$ bins.
% CHECK-FINAL [C56]: PSOBV-RSDVNS W >= 40, L == 0, p_Holm < 0.05, whole 90 % CI below -\NEqMargin; [C14]: PSOBV-RSVNS L == 0, PSOBV-BVNS L <= 2 ("clearly beats every control")
% CHECK-FINAL [C53]: SSABV-RSDVNS W == 0, case-mean losses > wins, p_Holm < 0.05 against SSA-VNS; CHECK-EXTRA [X33]: not at cluster level ("not the farm clusters"; never "worse"); [X49]: HL > 0, 95 % CI \NXHLSSAVNSvsRSDVNSCI; [X53]: lower 90 % limits above -m at seed and case level, not at cluster level ("ruled out at the seed and the case level"); CHECK-FINAL [C54]: 90 % case CI not inside +-m (equivalence not shown); CHECK-EXTRA [X54]: seed level equivalent, case/cluster not ("inconclusive")
% CHECK-FINAL [C55]: LXBV-RSDVNS L > W, p_Holm < 0.05, DL > 0; CHECK-EXTRA [X34]: CR p 0.087 ("worse", not "clearly worse"); CHECK-FINAL [C15]/[C51]: SSABV-RSVNS small gain, not equivalent; [C50]: LX-SSA-VNS vs RS-VNS equivalent (\NEqLXSSAVNSvsRSVNSCI)
% CHECK-DIR [F16]: 1-deg: SSABV and LXBV higher case-mean loss than RSDVNS (p < 0.05), PSOBV better than RSDVNS, RSVNS, BVNS (p < 0.05); [F17]: 1-deg SSABV-RSVNS p >= 0.05 (\NFAblSSAVNSvsRSVNSOneP)

\emph{Choice of Phase~1.} With the same VNS phase, PSO-VNS is significantly better than SSA-VNS and LX-SSA-VNS in most cases and significantly worse in none (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}). At the switch, its global best is more often feasible and, when feasible, has a lower wake loss (Table~\ref{S-tab:switch}). Whether PSO helps mainly by faster feasibility or by better exploration remains open (cf.\ the feasible starts in Section~\ref{sec:feasinit}).
% CHECK-FINAL [C16]: PSOBV-SSABV, PSOBV-LXBV: L == 0, W >= 34; switch feasibility higher and switch loss lower for PSOBV

\emph{LX-SSA as published.} LX-SSA costs $2N_p$ evaluations per iteration (Section~\ref{sec:ssa}) and so runs half as many iterations as SSA. As published, it makes the salp swarm worse, alone and after VNS (last two contrasts of Table~\ref{tab:ablation}). An ablation on the twelve split cases separates the Laplace step from this cost (Section~\ref{S-sec:S-xp-lap}, Table~\ref{S-tab:S-xp-laplace}):
\begin{enumerate}
\item without its redundant re-evaluation, LX-SSA has a lower wake loss, so part of its deficit against SSA is its doubled cost;
\item at the cost of SSA, the Laplace candidate gives no lower wake loss than the SSA midpoint;
\item a uniform step with the same mean absolute value performs like the Laplace step, and smaller scales are worse.
\end{enumerate}
With twelve cases these are estimates, not confirmed effects: none of the Holm-adjusted case-mean Wilcoxon tests against LX-SSA is significant.% CHECK-FINAL [C17]: LX-SSA as published worse (never "Laplace step harmful"): LXSSA-SSA W == 0; SSABV-LXBV L == 0; case means favour SSA and SSA-VNS (p < 0.05, so also Holm)

% tab:equivalence (all 18 pairs) moved to the supplementary material (Table S-tab:equivalence); summary: tab:equiv-main in 06_results.tex

\emph{Practical equivalence.} Table~\ref{S-tab:equivalence} of the supplementary material gives, for all 18 pairs, the TOST decision~\cite{Lakens2017} at $\pm0.05$~pp, the minimal margin and the Bayesian signed-rank probabilities~\cite{Benavoli2017}; Table~\ref{tab:equiv-main} summarizes the primary questions and the sampling-control contrasts. At the case level (a sensitivity analysis):
\begin{enumerate}
\item only PSO-VNS vs.\ PSO (a primary question; Section~\ref{sec:psovns-pso}) and LX-SSA-VNS vs.\ RS-VNS (exploratory, like all other pairs) are equivalent; no pair is equivalent over the scenario clusters;
\item PSO-VNS gains more than the margin over every control, as does the VNS phase after SSA and LX-SSA;
\item LX-SSA as published is worse than SSA by more than the margin;
\item over RS-VNS, a negligible SSA gain is the more probable outcome, though equivalence is not shown.
\end{enumerate}
% CHECK-FINAL [C49]: PSO-VNS vs PSO equivalent at the case level; [C50]: LX-SSA-VNS vs RS-VNS equivalent; CHECK-EXTRA [X55]: no pair of tab:equivalence equivalent at the cluster level; CHECK-FINAL [C56]/[C14]: PSO-VNS 90 % CIs vs RSD-VNS, RS-VNS ([-0.479, -0.324]) and VNS ([-0.545, -0.386]) below -m; [C33]: VNS phase for SSA / LX-SSA, 90 % CIs [-0.368, -0.226] / [-0.505, -0.293] below -m; [C17]: LX-SSA vs SSA 90 % CI [0.104, 0.206] above +m; [C51]: SSA-VNS vs RS-VNS not equivalent, P(rope) \NBaySSAVNSvsRSVNSRope > P(better) \NBaySSAVNSvsRSVNSBetter; CHECK-EXTRA [X38]: all 18 pairs of tab:equivalence (CIs, TOST, m_min, verdict, Bayes) reproduced from the per-run data
For SSA-VNS vs.\ RSD-VNS the primary question is one-sided: does the SSA phase give a better start than disc sampling by a relevant amount? The interval rules this out at the seed and the case level, equivalence is not shown, and the Bayesian posterior and the all-run paired outcome (Section~\ref{sec:archive-reliability}) point against SSA-VNS. We therefore claim only that an SSA phase gives no gain of practical size over disc sampling at $4D$ (and $5D$, not $6D$; Section~\ref{sec:spacing}), not that it is equivalent or worse; LX-SSA-VNS is worse.

% CHECK-EXTRA [X53]: lower 90 % limits of SSA-VNS - RSD-VNS above -m at the seed and case levels, not at the cluster level (stated in the swarm paragraph above); [X33]: case-level disadvantage not at cluster level (never "worse"); CHECK-FINAL [C54]: equivalence not shown at the case level, P(SSA-VNS better) < 0.01; [C55]: LX-SSA-VNS worse than RSD-VNS, CI above 0, not equivalent

\subsection{Budget Split}
\label{sec:split}
% Page budget: kept in the supplementary material: \AblTable{tab:split}
Of the five splits tested on twelve cases, from $\omega=0.25$ to plain PSO ($\omega=1$; Table~\ref{S-tab:split}), $\omega=0.75$ has the lowest mean wake loss and average rank, and $\omega=0.9$ does not beat it.
% CHECK-FINAL [C47]: 0.75 best of the \NSplitNSettings settings (lowest mean loss and rank); [C59]: omega = 0.9 vs 0.75 W = L = 0, case-mean p >= 0.05 ("does not beat"); CHECK-EXTRA [X36]: 0.9 beats 0.75 at no level. SWEVO: the split table is back in the main text (tab:split; logged in optA/sw/moved.md)
Per case (Table~\ref{S-tab:split-cases}):
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\omega=0.75$ has a lower mean loss than $\omega=0.5$ in eleven cases (significantly in four) and a significantly higher one in none, and $\omega=0.25$ is significantly worse than $\omega=0.5$ in five cases;
\item plain PSO has a higher mean loss than $\omega=0.75$, so the best split is interior;
\item $\omega=0.9$ differs from $\omega=0.75$ in no case, and its mean loss lies between those of $\omega=0.75$ and $\omega=0.5$.
\end{enumerate}
The results of items~1 and~2 hold in all six farm clusters. We keep the preset $\omega=0.5$, which may slightly understate PSO-VNS; a larger split is a candidate to confirm on the target problem.
% CHECK-FINAL [C18]: 75 % lower loss than 50 % in >= 10 of 12 cases, 50 % never significantly better than 75 %, 25 % never significantly better than 50 %; [C47]: omega = 1 higher mean loss than 0.75 ("interior"); CHECK-EXTRA [X37]: 0.5 > 0.25, 0.75 > 0.5, 0.75 > 1 in all six clusters; CHECK-FINAL [C59]: 0.9 vs 0.75 W = L = 0, case-mean p >= 0.05, higher loss than 0.75, lower than 0.5 and omega = 1

\subsection{Summary of the Ablation}
\label{sec:abl-summary}
The ablation shows that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the PSO phase is the component that matters: with the same VNS phase, PSO-VNS beats VNS alone, both random-sampling controls, SSA-VNS and LX-SSA-VNS (Section~\ref{sec:abl-swarm});
\item the VNS phase, at this budget essentially one compass-search descent, adds little after PSO (not significant, practically equivalent at the case level) but clearly improves SSA and LX-SSA (Section~\ref{sec:abl-vns});
\item at the benchmark spacing $4D$, the salp-swarm phases give no gain of practical size over sampling in the farm disc, and LX-SSA-VNS is worse than RSD-VNS (Section~\ref{sec:abl-swarm});
\item square sampling (RS-VNS) is a weak control, mainly because random layouts rarely satisfy the spacing constraint (Section~\ref{sec:abl-controls});
\item LX-SSA as published is worse than SSA, in part because of its doubled cost per iteration (Section~\ref{sec:abl-swarm});
\item the best split is interior: $\omega=0.75$ was best of the five splits tested, so the preset $\omega=0.5$ may slightly understate PSO-VNS (Section~\ref{sec:split}).
\end{enumerate}

% end optA/sw/07_ablation.tex

\section{Conclusions and Future Directions}
\label{sec:discussion}
\subsection{Summary of the Results}
\label{sec:farm-summary}

Table~\ref{tab:farm-summary} summarizes the nine methods over the four wind farm types. PSO-VNS ranks first in six of the nine settings and second or third in the other three, in which GA ranks first: the Horns Rev~1 block at 30,030 evaluations and both IEA37 budgets with 36 turbines. PSO-VNS ranks ahead of PSO in every setting and is significantly better than PSO in every site setting, but not on the benchmark, where the two are practically equivalent at the seed level (Section~\ref{sec:psovns-pso}). PSO-VNS is significantly worse than one method in four settings (GA three times; MS-SLSQP on the Lillgrund block at 6,030 evaluations) and never worse than more than one.

\subsection{Conclusions}
\label{sec:conclusion}
\label{sec:disc-where}
\label{sec:disc-practice}
We proposed PSO-VNS, a two-phase method for the WFLOP that runs the standard PSO, with the constriction coefficients of Clerc and Kennedy, for half of the evaluation budget and then a basic VNS from the best layout found. We compared it with eight methods (PSO, SSA-VNS, SSA, LX-SSA, DE, GA, VNS and MS-SLSQP) on four types of wind farm, with equal evaluation budgets, seed-paired runs, random starts and one penalty and repair for all methods. The findings are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Type~I, Kusiak--Song benchmark (68 cases; Section~\ref{sec:farm-I}).} PSO-VNS has the best average rank, followed by PSO and GA; it is not significantly different from these two and significantly better than the other six methods. Its average advantage over PSO is small: within the 0.05~pp margin on this fixed benchmark and under exchangeable-case sensitivity, not under scenario-cluster sensitivity. Case by case, the differences go both ways: PSO-VNS has the lower wake loss in all ten Data Set~II cases with $N\ge10$ (post hoc), whereas every Data Set~I case in which the two differ by more than the margin, among the denser layouts, favors PSO. On the all-run endpoint, PSO-VNS is better than PSO more often than not.
% CHECK-FINAL [C01]: PSO-VNS best average rank; CHECK-EXTRA [X39]: seed/case equivalent, cluster not; CHECK-FINAL [C32] + CHECK-EXTRA [X19]: DS II N >= 10, all-family Holm; CHECK-EXTRA [X43]: 19/68 beyond margin, both directions; all \NXHetDsINPSO DS I cases beyond m favour PSO; [X25]: mean gain < 0.05 % AEP; CHECK (manual, 2026-10-04): all-run score 0.551 (tab:S-sa-allrun)
\item \emph{Type~II, Horns Rev~1 block (Section~\ref{sec:farm-II}).} PSO-VNS ranks first at 6,030 evaluations and is feasible in all 30 runs (PSO: 19); at 30,030 evaluations GA ranks first and PSO-VNS second. The block (16 of 80 turbines, $4D$ instead of $7D$ spacing, no cables, loads or navigation) tests optimizers, not the installed design: with 1$^\circ$ direction bins, no optimized layout exceeds the installed block.
% CHECK-EXTRA [X52]: Horns Rev 30/30 vs 19/30, McNemar p 9.8e-4 (one site, random starts); CHECK-FINAL [C08]/[C20]; CHECK-DIR [F21]: 0 of 961 feasible runs (ten methods and GA) above the installed block at 1 deg; CHECK-FINAL [C62]: no \NHR...Above macro used; CHECK (manual, 2026-10-04): GA first at 30,030 on Horns Rev 1 with 5-deg, 1-deg (+0.48, p 2.8e-4) and PyWake NOJ (+0.43, p 4.2e-4) (tab:S-sa-hr-pool, tab:S-sa-hr-eval; analysis/rev3_sites.json)
\item \emph{Type~III, Lillgrund block (Section~\ref{sec:farm-III}).} PSO-VNS ranks first by mean AEP at both budgets and is feasible more often than PSO. At 6,030 evaluations, however, MS-SLSQP is more reliable (feasible in all 30 runs, PSO-VNS in 17, PSO in none), and the AEP lead of PSO-VNS over the runners-up is not significant with 1$^\circ$ bins.
% CHECK (manual, 2026-10-04): Lillgrund 17/30 vs 0/30 and 30/30 vs 19/30; at 1 deg PSO-VNS highest mean at 30,030 but p_Holm 0.16 / 0.58 / 0.58 vs SSA-VNS / VNS / MS-SLSQP, the runners-up (tab:S-sa-lg-eval)
\item \emph{Type~IV, IEA37 Case Study~1 (Section~\ref{sec:farm-IV}).} PSO-VNS ranks first with 16 turbines and GA with 36 turbines, at both budgets. On this piecewise-smooth model, SLSQP with exact gradients, alone or after a PSO phase, does better than every metaheuristic compared, with feasibility labels as reported by the runs. For smooth, differentiable wake models, our results thus favor gradient-based optimization, possibly started from a swarm; the metaheuristic comparisons of this paper are relevant where the objective is discontinuous, as with the top-hat wake of the benchmark, or gradients are unavailable.
% CHECK (manual, see 06d_type4 sec:iea-grad): exact-gradient variant first in both scenarios and budgets, labels as reported (220 of 240 undecidable)
\item \emph{Ablation (Section~\ref{sec:ablation}).} The PSO phase is the component that matters: it gives the second phase, essentially one compass-search descent, a clearly better start than random sampling in the farm. The salp-swarm first phases give no gain of practical size over random sampling in the farm at the benchmark spacing $4D$ (at $6D$ the SSA phase does gain), and LX-SSA as published makes them worse. After PSO, the VNS phase adds little on the benchmark.
% CHECK-FINAL [C53]/[C54]: no SSA gain over disc sampling = "in the farm"; [C55]: LX-SSA-VNS worse; [C17]: LX-SSA as published worse; [C56]/[C14]/[C44]: PSO phase beats both controls; CHECK-EXTRA [X53]: gain > margin ruled out across seeds and cases (one-sided); CHECK-DIAG [D08]: essentially one descent
\item \emph{Robustness (Sections~\ref{sec:beyond} and~\ref{sec:robustness}).} On the benchmark, PSO-VNS keeps first place under a cubic power curve, when its layouts are re-evaluated with 1$^\circ$ direction bins and when the search itself uses 1$^\circ$ bins (eight cases), where its advantage over PSO exceeds the margin; under the Gaussian wake with 15$^\circ$ bins, PSO ranks narrowly first. Finer bins raise the wake loss of every method, because layouts optimized under a coarse rose exploit the gaps between its bins; gains found under a coarse rose should therefore be checked, and preferably obtained, at a fine resolution. The lead of PSO-VNS depends on random starts (from feasible starts, VNS and GA rank ahead of it), on the budget (with twenty times the benchmark budget, GA ranks first and PSO-VNS second), on the spacing (at $5D$, GA ranks first) and partly on constraint handling: with Deb's feasibility rules and box clipping, the order of the leading methods changes; with radial projection, it is kept, but the all-run advantage of PSO-VNS over PSO is not significant.
% CHECK-DIR [F06]/[F09] + CHECK-FINAL [C21]/[C22]: PSO-VNS first at 15/5/1 deg (Jensen), cubic curves, Gaussian 1 deg; PSO first only under Gaussian 15 deg; CHECK-DIR [F03]-[F05], [F07]: every method's loss rises with 1-deg bins, coarse bins exploited; CHECK-FINAL [C28]/[C29]: the leader changes from feasible starts (VNS rank, GA loss) and at 120,030 (GA); spacing 5D: GA best rank (rev5_ga_secondary_report.md)
% CHECK (manual, 2026-10-04): direct 1-deg: PSO-VNS rank 1.12, PSO-VNS - PSO -0.194 pp, case 90% [-0.272, -0.114] beyond -0.05 (rev3_fine.json); direct 1-deg optimization lowers the 1-deg loss on the same seeds (analysis/rev3_fine.json); constraint study, 6 cases x 5 methods, projection: conditional dL -0.095 pp [-0.162, -0.037], all-run 0.561 ns (tab:S-sa-constraint)
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Limitations}
\label{sec:limits}
\label{sec:disc-recs}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Constraint handling and configuration.} All methods minimize one penalty, which adds boundary violations in m$^2$ to spacing violations in m ($\mu=10^{10}$), with box clipping; the conclusions partly depend on this choice (Section~\ref{sec:boundary}). The weaknesses observed for SSA, LX-SSA and DE therefore characterize these implementations under this penalty and repair, not their algorithm families. DE is untuned (its low feasibility depends on the repair), and MS-SLSQP uses finite differences on a discontinuous objective.
% CHECK-DIAG [D01]/[D02]: instrumented runs reproduce the stored runs; generated \NFeasDE ("depends on the repair": DE feasibility 33.3 % -> 80.0 % with Deb + projection, rev3_constraint.json); CHECK-FINAL [C17]: LX-SSA as published
\item \emph{Local search.} At 6,030 evaluations the VNS phase is essentially one truncated compass-search descent, so the ablation concerns this local search, not VNS in general.
% CHECK-DIAG [D08]-[D10]: truncated compass search
\item \emph{Model.} The benchmark uses the top-hat Jensen wake, with its non-physical upstream deficit (Section~\ref{sec:model}), and a linear power curve, so its objective is a benchmark value, not farm quality; except in the direct 1$^\circ$ study (eight cases), the search used coarse wind roses. Turbulence and loads (critical at $4D$), wake steering, blockage, terrain, cables, electrical losses and costs are outside our scope; all AEP values are gross.
% CHECK-FINAL [C21]: cubic curve lowers the power, ranking intact; CHECK-DIR [F32]/[F34]: PyWake 661.39 vs ours 666.75 GWh/yr, wake loss -0.72 pp, cause C_T at free-stream speed
\item \emph{Farms and budgets.} The benchmark farms are circular, with a prescribed $N\le15$ and $4D$ spacing; measured wind climates enter only through two 16-turbine blocks, on which the feasibility advantage of PSO-VNS rests and whose AEP rankings partly depend on the evaluator; the 64-turbine IEA37 scenario was not run. The rankings are those of small budgets: at 120,030 evaluations GA overtakes PSO-VNS on the six largest benchmark cases (Section~\ref{sec:budget}).
% CHECK-FINAL [C45] (budget study: SSA-VNS ahead of PSO at 120,030); CHECK-EXTRA [X52]: Horns Rev feasibility (one site, random starts); CHECK (manual, 2026-10-04): GA lead at 30,030 under 1-deg bins and PyWake NOJ (rev3_sites.json); Lillgrund PSO-VNS lead over the runners-up SSA-VNS, VNS, MS-SLSQP n.s. at 1 deg (p_Holm 0.16, 0.58, 0.58); still significant vs PSO, SSA, LX-SSA, DE, RSD-VNS
\item \emph{Method pool.} The pool lacks CMA-ES~\cite{HansenOstermeier2001}, L-SHADE~\cite{Tanabe2014}, adaptive GAs, ACO~\cite{Eroglu2012} and BBO~\cite{Bansal2017}, so ``first'' means first among the nine methods compared. Exact-gradient SLSQP was run only on IEA37, and 220 of its 240 strict feasibility labels cannot be decided from the stored coordinates (Section~\ref{sec:archive-reliability}; worst case: Table~\ref{S-tab:S-sa-feasworst}).
% CHECK-DIR [F44]: projected IEA37 gaps -1.93 / -6.23 % (30,030), feasibility convention matters on IEA37
\item \emph{Statistics.} The equivalence margin and the subgroup analyses are post hoc, and equivalence statements other than the two primary questions (Section~\ref{sec:setup-stats}) are exploratory. The 68 cases form only six clusters with nested $N$: an exact sign test over clusters cannot fall below $p=0.031$, cluster-robust intervals have 5 degrees of freedom, and the p-values describe this benchmark, not a population of farms. The studies on subsets of cases (budget, initialization, spacing, constraint handling and direct 1$^\circ$ optimization) are estimates for those cases, and their error rates are not controlled jointly.
% CHECK-FINAL [C49] + CHECK-EXTRA [X41]: post hoc margin, minimal margins; [X47]: density pattern (exploratory); [X10]: six clusters; [X11]: exact p = \NXClMinP; [X42]: CR2 5 d.f.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Future Directions}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Expensive wake models}: surrogate-assisted evaluation (graph neural network, data-driven or Kriging surrogates~\cite{Yang2023,Wang2024,Li2025}) would make equal-budget comparisons at higher fidelity affordable, if the surrogate error is reported next to the method differences.
\item \emph{Larger farms and more sites}, such as the IEA37 64-turbine scenario and the full Horns Rev~1 and Lillgrund farms.
\item \emph{Gradient-based methods} on smooth models of larger farms~\cite{Rodrigues2024}, and swarm--gradient hybrids, under budgets that charge every gradient.
\item \emph{Stronger reference methods and constraint handling}: CMA-ES, L-SHADE, tuned DE, and constraint handling designed for each method family.
\item \emph{Fine wind roses during optimization} on all cases, with loads and economic objectives.
\end{enumerate}
